Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In our previous posts - Easy FunctionGemma finetuning with Tunix on Google TPUs and Autonomous LLM post-training with Tunix on TPUs - we showed how Google Tunix streamlines Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and single-turn Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on Cloud TPUs. Real-world AI agents, however, rarely finish their job in a single turn. They have to reason over partial observations, invoke tools, observe environment feedback, and adapt their strategy across multiple steps. So in this post, we will walk through how to train a multi-turn, tool-using LLM agent using Tunix's agentic reinforcement learning pipeline on Cloud TPU v6e-8. You can find the complete code in this GitHub repository.

We will revisit the Plane Strike board game from an earlier TensorFlow/JAX reinforcement learning blog, replacing the custom MLP policy with Gemma 4 E2B in thinking mode on a single Cloud TPU v6e-8 host. Plane Strike is played on a 6x6 grid (A1 to F6) where one 8-cell plane is hidden in one of 4 orientations. Each turn, the agent sees the result of its previous strike and the raw visible board (. untried, X hit, O miss), thinks through candidate placements, and calls the strike(cell="...") tool to strike one cell. The game ends when all 8 plane cells are hit, or after 23 strikes.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Although google/gemma-4-E2B-it has decent spatial reasoning and tool calling capabilities, out of the box, it wins only 9.4% of games on held-out boards (averaging 22.44 strikes). After 100 steps of agentic GRPO with Tunix, its win rate reaches 95% while cutting the average game length to ~16 strikes.

We set up the multi-turn RL pipeline in four modular components:

Let's look at the key steps for each piece.

In env.py, we subclass Tunix's BaseTaskEnv to implement PlaneStrikeEnv. Each environment instance receives a task dictionary with a seed that fixes the hidden plane position so all 8 rollouts in a GRPO group play the exact same board:

On each turn, _step_impl evaluates the struck cell index against the hidden board, updates the visible grid, computes the step reward (summed into the trajectory-level reward over the episode), and returns EnvStepResult(observation, reward, done, info).

For standard multi-tool agents, tunix.rl.agentic also provides ready-to-use ToolEnvironment, ToolAgent and ToolManager abstractions out of the box so we don't have to write custom message/observation plumbing unless we need custom turn control.

In Gemma 4, tool schemas are declared in the system prompt inside <|tool>declaration:..., and a model turn that calls a tool ends with:

<|tool_response> is one of Gemma 4's native stop tokens: the model pauses there and waits for response:strike{value:<|"|>...<|"|>}, then continues generating its next thought and tool call within the same assistant turn.

In strike_tool.py, we define StrikeTool(BaseTool) and subclass Tunix's Gemma4ChatTemplateParser to append the tool declaration to the system prompt and render "tool" messages as in-turn responses:

In agent.py, PlaneStrikeAgent(ConversationAgentBase) translates between environment observations and model messages.

When the model finishes a turn, tool_strike ignores everything inside <|channel>thought... and parses the final answer after ; only a final answer with a single valid strike call counts as a valid board coordinate (otherwise INVALID_ACTION is returned). If the model stopped at <|tool_response>, the next board observation is appended with role="tool" so the model continues its turn:

In train_planestrike.py, we configure Tunix's asynchronous vllm rollout engine, RLEngine, and GRPOLearner:

Multi-turn agentic RL is often bottlenecked by variable-length rollout generation, so we set rollout_vllm_server_mode to true to enable continuous asynchronous multi-turn rollouts via vllm-tpu and enable_prefix_caching to true to true to cache the growing multi-turn conversation history. Since we are using a v6e-8 single host, the actor model, the reference model, and the rollout workers share the 8 chips.

We then pass our actor, tokenizer, ClusterConfig, GRPOConfig (loss_algo="gspo-token", advantage_estimator="rloo", exact_token_continuity=True to preserve exact token IDs across multi-turn rollouts without retokenization drift), agent, and environment into RLEngine and PlaneStrikeGRPOLearner:

All of these enable us to launch the 100-step training job with:

Over 100 steps (8 boards x 8 games per step), the agent learns both reliable tool calling and spatial deduction:

With Tunix's tunix.rl.agentic abstractions, vllm-tpu, and GRPOLearner, multi-turn, tool-using agentic RL on Cloud TPUs is easy and efficient. Check out the planestrike-gemma4-rl GitHub repository and read the Tunix documentation to train your own agentic model with Tunix and Google TPUs.

Controversial GTA 6 Easter Egg Will Annoy Millions of Gamers

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A physical game joins NFT jokes as relics of the past included in GTA 6

Grand Theft Auto 6 is now out everywhere, and players are discovering just how much this game is filled to the brim with an overwhelming amount of things to do. In the game’s opening hours, you’ll land a place on the water, steal a car from a police impound, get into a car chase, and more. There are a million little things to do, like playing video games inside GTA 6 itself.

Early in the game, Cal, a friend of co-protagonist Jason, spends an afternoon gaming with his buddy. Ironically, he does something that IRL players can’t do with GTA 6 itself: bring a physical game disc with him.

As GTA 6’s nearly 20 million players (at the time of this writing) know, GTA 6 is digital-only. “Physical” copies simply contain a digital download code in a box. As such, you can’t bring your copy of GTA 6 to a friend’s house to show them the game like you could with previous GTA games, or like how Cal introduces Jason to Tenebrae Lux, a very artsy game that spoofs Jonathan Blow’s The Witness. (Jason’s not impressed; he’d rather go kill zombies.)

The two then have a party-filled night together that ends with them drunkenly fighting over who gets to sleep in the bed, which then cuts to the both of them sharing the bed in their undies. It’s a surprisingly wholesome sequence that includes Jason opening up about the strains in his relationship with Lucia on the eve of her getting released from prison. The moments of genuine friendship between Cal and Jason contrast well to the rest of GTA 6, which is all about, ya know, stealing drugs from rednecks, evading death-by-gator, running away from cops, and all other kinds of wild shenanigans.

Trading games, bringing a game disc to a hang sesh with the boys, introducing a new game to a friend by popping your copy into their console — for years, sharing games was easy because they were all on discs or other physical formats. When I reviewed the Gears of War remaster last year (with a digital copy), it reminded me how I first played the original game by trading my copy of Assassin’s Creed with a friend for his copy of Gears. Fast-forward to this year, and I’m playing Pokémon Pokopia by borrowing my partner’s copy. (OK, maybe I stole it from her Switch 2 case, who can say.)

PlayStation once famously demonstrated how to share used games in a 2013 video intended as a dig toward the always-online Xbox One. That joke has aged like milk in the wake of Sony’s announcement that it will cease manufacturing physical discs for PlayStation games in January 2028. Sony’s news hit just a short time after Rockstar Games announced its own digital-only plans for GTA 6 back in June.

Sony’s move prompted a wave of angry commenters spamming every PlayStation promotional video and blog post with disdain ever since. Some PlayStation owners tried to wrangle together a “no disc, no buy” blackout campaign where they wouldn’t use PSN, make purchases at the PlayStation Store, or basically engage with their PlayStation console in any way. That blackout had no effect on Sony. In fact, Sony’s digital game sales have continued to grow since the announcement.

The outrage over the decline of physical games clearly hasn’t had an impact on Rockstar’s GTA 6 either. It broke preorder records, despite there being no real need to preorder a copy — they’re all digital, so Sony and Microsoft won’t be running out. This isn’t 2009, when you had to preorder a physical copy of Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 2 to make sure you could pwn some newbz day one. (And snag a sleek poster in the process.)

Though we can’t say with certainty yet, it seems likely GTA 6 will end up as 2026’s top-selling game, in spite of the uproar around its lack of a disc version. Yet none of the eleventy billion people who buy it will be able to share it with their buds in the way that Cal shared a disc version of Tenebrae Lux with Jason.

Rockstar’s magnum opus searches for love and catharsis in desperate times — and finds them

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Cosmos 3 Edge, Part 3: SO-101 Sim-to-Real and Robot Control on Jetson Thor

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at blog.cavedu.com • AI-assisted

In the first two articles, we introduced Cosmos 3 Edge on Jetson and explored the Physical AI workflow from visual understanding to action reasoning. The remaining step is to turn an AI decision into a machine's movement: the action in perceive, reason, and act must change something in the physical environment.

NVIDIA's SO-101 Sim-to-Real Learning Path offers a useful development route, from calibration and simulation through data collection, VLA training, autonomous tests on a real robot, and Cosmos-based dataset augmentation.

One distinction is essential: the SO-101 learning path uses GR00T VLA as its main robot policy. Cosmos is used for dataset augmentation, not as a direct replacement for GR00T in that course. NVIDIA's separate Cosmos 3 Edge route demonstrates a post-trained robot policy running on Jetson AGX Thor.

This final article connects these complementary pieces of the Physical AI ecosystem.

These links open the original CAVEDU articles in Traditional Chinese.

A conventional robot controller accepts low-level inputs such as joint positions, velocities, or other control signals and drives the motors accordingly. It does not, by itself, understand a request such as “Pick up the industrial object and put it in the yellow container.”

That apparently simple task involves several questions:

The NVIDIA GR00T architecture combines visual observations with language for task understanding, followed by action output for the robot.

A Physical AI system connects sensors, perception, world understanding, a policy, actions, and the robot. It also needs feedback. After every action, the environment has changed. New observations must influence the next decision. This is closed-loop operation.

Train an SO-101 Robot From Sim-to-Real With NVIDIA Isaac is more than a guide to moving an arm. It describes a workflow involving:

The learning path also includes Cosmos augmentation and autonomous tests. Choosing a model is only one part of the challenge. Data, training, simulation, and physical evaluation must form a coherent process.

At a 333 Robotics Community meetup in September 2026, the original author made a related observation about LeRobot: the robot hardware is not the whole story. Data and policy are central to the ecosystem.

Physical AI draws attention to the AI model, but practical robot development often starts with calibration. Both the SO-101 follower and its teleoperation leader arm need calibration so the system knows their joint states and operating ranges.

If the model's representation of the robot does not match the physical mechanism, a more capable model will not simply erase that mismatch. The system's understanding must align with the actual robot body.

Testing directly on a real robot can be costly. Failures can damage equipment, disrupt a test setup, or stop a production line. Isaac Sim provides an environment for teleoperation, data collection, and policy evaluation before transferring a candidate policy to the real robot.

The development sequence is simulation, data collection, policy training, evaluation, and physical deployment. Simulation helps explore and test the policy; real-world evaluation remains necessary.

A policy that performs well in simulation may still fail on the physical robot. The source groups sim-to-real differences into four categories:

Many small differences are difficult to represent fully. See the learning-path discussion referenced by the source.

The original article identifies this image as AI-generated.

In the SO-101 learning path, Cosmos appears as a dataset-augmentation strategy. The idea is to increase the diversity of training data rather than rely only on a limited set of demonstrations.

Simulation demonstrations, real data, and generated variations can contribute to a broader training dataset. Text-to-video and image-to-video workflows can introduce visual variation, including backgrounds, lighting, appearances, and environments.

The goal is not attractive video for its own sake. The variations must help the trained policy generalize to different conditions, while preserving the task-relevant information needed for training.

The original article also points readers to Cosmos 3 Nano on NVIDIA's model platform. Cosmos-family capabilities extend beyond visually realistic generation to relationships among observations, motion, and actions; the exact interface depends on the selected model and workflow.

Returning to Cosmos 3 Edge, NVIDIA describes another path toward edge robot control:

See the model introduction and on-device robot-control post-training guide.

These are distinct roles within the ecosystem. In the SO-101 course, GR00T VLA provides the manipulation-policy route and Cosmos supplies augmentation. The separate Cosmos 3 Edge demonstration deploys a post-trained policy on Thor. Combining their names does not automatically create a supported plug-and-play integration.

In the cited on-device workflow, the policy server runs on Jetson AGX Thor. This brings policy inference close to the robot rather than requiring every observation and action decision to travel through a remote AI server.

Jetson is therefore more than a camera-processing box. It can serve as the robot's onboard AI computer, subject to the workload's memory, compute, software, and integration requirements.

As discussed in Part 2, a policy can predict a sequence of actions for the robot to execute. The system observes, predicts an action chunk, begins execution, observes again, and replans.

The key question is whether inference timing and action execution fit together. Repeatedly pausing the robot while waiting for a large model to decide on one small movement is different from generating motion segments and updating them through feedback.

Chunking does not remove the need for a controller, robot-specific action conversion, or validation. It provides a way to organize policy predictions across time.

Both aim to connect perception, decision-making, and physical action. A working robot still needs cameras, perception, a policy, actions, a controller, the physical mechanism, and feedback.

The SO-101 workflow tackles the broader development problem: how can a policy trained and evaluated in simulation remain useful on real hardware? Cosmos 3 Edge demonstrates a path for world/action modeling to become an edge robot policy.

The current learning path should not be read as “Download Cosmos 3 Edge, connect an SO-101, and press Run.” The policy, data, embodiment, camera arrangement, and controller must be matched and evaluated.

Even with a model, data, Jetson, and an arm, the practical question remains: What success rate do we obtain after the real robot starts moving?

This is why real evaluation matters. Simulation measures performance in the simulated environment. Deployment on the physical robot reveals how the system handles the actual world.

Part 1 introduced how Cosmos 3 Edge helps interpret observations. Part 2 connected world understanding to action decisions. This final article places those capabilities inside a full robot-development workflow.

The destination is a continuous loop: observe the world, understand the task, choose actions, execute them, and observe the changed world again. Answering a visual question or generating an impressive video is only one part of that process.

Alongside task success, the original article highlights intervention rate: how often must a human step in per 100 cycles? A system requiring frequent rescue is not yet a dependable production tool.

Completing a task once may be luck. Repeating it 10, 50, or 100 times under different lighting, object positions, and environments provides more useful evidence. Better data, more accurate simulation, stronger policies, faster edge inference, and reliable closed-loop control all matter.

Physical AI depends on a robust workflow connecting robot, simulation, data, VLA, Cosmos, and real-robot evaluation. The next topic is preparing OpenUSD assets for simulation—the bridge toward SimReady content.

English translation and editorial clarification of CAVEDU's original article. Original author: CAVEDU 阿吉. Original images and diagrams retain their source labels and may contain Traditional Chinese. The sim-to-real gap illustration is identified above as AI-generated.

The 7-Gate Production Launch Checklist for Solo Developers

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A solo launch rarely dies from a missing feature. In my experience watching one-person products ship (and in the ones I've broken myself), it dies from one of three things — and all three are testable in an afternoon:

Every gate below exists to catch one of those three before a stranger finds it for you.

A gate is a check with a written exit criterion and an artifact that proves it passed. Not a feeling. Not "it worked when I tried it." An artifact:

If you cannot point at the artifact, the gate is open and the launch is a bet.

Gates 1–5 and 7 are blocking. Gate 6 is soft only because a broken launch with no comms template still launches — it just hurts more.

"Freeze the scope" sounds like project management. It is actually a testing requirement: you can only test a fixed set of behaviour, and every feature you add after the smoke pass invalidates part of that pass.

The rule I use is mechanical: from the moment the second full smoke pass starts (T-1), the deployable artifact changes only for security fixes, data-loss fixes, and the launch checklist itself. Anything else is a commit on a branch, merged after launch.

Write the freeze as one page you can check a build against: what ships (3–7 user-visible capabilities, each with one sentence and one URL), what explicitly does not ship, who can use it, what it costs, and what "working" means — the single number you will check on day seven. A launch scope without a success number becomes a launch you cannot evaluate.

Every "it works locally" story is a story about a difference. The audit's job is to find the differences on purpose, before a stranger finds them by accident.

For each variable and key, capture five things: name, purpose, where it is set, whether staging has the same value, and when it was last rotated. Then follow the rules that prevent the usual damage:

The highest-value ten minutes of the whole audit is the test-key search: grep production config for sk_test, sandbox, localhost, 127.0.0.1, :3000, example.com, changeme. A payment provider left in test mode is invisible for days — the page looks fine, nobody can pay.

A deploy that crashes in ten seconds is infinitely better than one that serves 500s for an hour.

Also check the boring, cheap, catastrophic things: apex domain resolves as well as www, the certificate covers both, and there is exactly one HTTPS redirect hop. Lower the TTL on records you might change to 300 seconds 48 hours before launch. If your domain sends mail, confirm SPF, DKIM and DMARC before the first launch email — that email is often the first your domain has ever sent, and it lands in spam.

A backup file that has never been restored is a rumour. The only claim you can make with confidence at launch is: I restored last night's backup into a scratch database and counted the rows.

A restore that takes 90 minutes is not an emergency plan, it is an outage with a schedule — and knowing that in advance changes how you launch.

The other half of data safety is the expand–contract pattern, the single most useful discipline for one-person teams. Split every risky schema change into three releases: expand (add the new column, nullable, nothing reads it yet), backfill and switch (write both, read new, flip back by changing one line), contract (drop the old column a week later). At every point the previous release still works, which is what makes a rollback a redeploy instead of a restore.

And keep an irreversible list: dropping a column, rewriting values in place, deleting storage objects, rotating a key whose old value is still in use, sending an email you cannot recall. If the list is not empty, either split the release or write down explicitly why you accepted it.

Unit tests prove functions behave. Smoke tests prove a person can get from the front door to value without help — and on launch day the second one is what matters.

Three properties make a smoke suite useful: it is twelve paths, not two hundred (fast enough to run three times in the last two days); it runs in the same conditions as the stranger (fresh account, private window, phone viewport, no admin flag, real network); and it is recorded with a timestamp and the conditions — "pass, 14:20, private window, phone, cellular" is evidence.

The twelve paths, chosen because they are where solo products break for someone else while working for their author:

Path 7 is the one most often faked. A webhook that fires correctly once is not the same as an endpoint that is idempotent, signature-checked and tolerant of out-of-order delivery. Replay it twice and confirm the second delivery changes nothing.

Conditions that produce false passes, and what to do instead:

Add a column to your checklist notes called conditions. "Pass — private window, fresh account, 5G" is worth ten times "pass".

Set the standard in one sentence: if the site breaks, you know within five minutes without looking at it.

If you install only two things before launch, install error tracking and one uptime check — then force both to fire, so you know the path from failure to your phone actually works. That round trip, from your deliberate error to your screen, is the artifact.

Two settings matter more than the rest: environment tagging (without it, staging errors page you at 2am) and noise filters (an error tracker that sends forty notifications a day gets muted in week two, and then it is not monitoring at all).

Check the URL a customer actually uses, not /health on the internal port. A health endpoint that returns 200 while the database is down is worse than no check, because it reports green through an outage:

Have the health check do a one-row read against the database and return non-200 when it fails. That is the difference between "the server is running" and "the product works".

And keep an alert budget: maximum three alerts before launch (site down, error rate above threshold, one business counter at zero). Every alert must be actionable at 2am, and every alert needs one line written next to it: if this fires, do X. An alert without a written response is a notification, and notifications get ignored.

Most launch pain is not technical. It is a vague message arriving at the worst possible moment, and you composing a reply while you are also debugging. Write the three messages before launch, so on the day you paste instead of think:

And when a bug report is vague, answer with structure: acknowledge it, ask the two questions that make it reproducible (what did you click right before it happened, and roughly what time it was), and commit to a time you can actually meet. Then keep that time, even if the answer is "not fixed yet, here is what I know."

On launch day you will be too close to judge. Decide now, in writing, with numbers:

Solo means the authority is never in question — only the threshold.

Before launch, answer this on paper: if I roll back this deploy, does the database still work with the old code? If the answer is no, your only path is forward, and you should know that before you press the button rather than discover it at T+40.

Ask these out loud, in this order, in the last hour before you press launch. Any "no" is a delay, not a discussion.

Question 5 is the one that gets skipped and the one that costs the most.

For a small launch — a landing page with a checkout, a small tool, a first paid tier — this is the minimum defensible pass. Twelve checks, ninety minutes, no gate skipped entirely:

If you only do one of these, do number 4. A launch with a tested restore is a bad afternoon at worst. A launch without one is a company-ending event that happens slowly.

This is the shape of a 30-page runbook I put together for one-person launches — seven gates, 63 checks and 13 editable files (checklist CSV, env audit, gate map, smoke-test plan and script, restore drill, migration/rollback plan, launch-day script, incident comms templates, week-one review). It is $7 here if you want the fill-in-the-blank version instead of rebuilding it: The Solo Launch Runbook.

If you are freelancing rather than shipping a product, the free starter pack is probably the better first download — ten AI prompts for proposals, scoping and client updates: 10 Essential AI Prompts for Freelancers (free).

Either way: write the artifact down. A checklist you have not marked up is a wish list, and a launch you cannot point at evidence for is a bet.

GBase Database Online Dbspace Expansion: Add a Chunk or Extend an Existing One?

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Summary: When a dbspace runs low on capacity, GBase Database(GBase 8s) provides two expansion approaches: adding a new chunk or extending an existing chunk. This tutorial explains both methods, their commands, prerequisites, differences, and post-expansion verification steps to help DBAs expand database storage safely.

When database storage approaches its capacity limit, adding more disk space is not the only consideration. DBAs also need to decide how that capacity should be incorporated into the existing storage structure.

In GBase Database(GBase 8s), there are two approaches:

Both approaches can expand an existing dbspace, but they use different commands and have different prerequisites.

Adding a new chunk is a straightforward way to increase dbspace capacity.

One important rule: the page size of a new chunk must match the page size of its target dbspace.

For example, a dbspace created with a 16KB page size requires compatible 16KB chunk storage. You cannot use a different page size when adding a chunk.

GBase Database also supports adding a chunk through the SQL Administration API:

Both methods add a chunk to the existing dbspace.

Do not stop at a successful command response. Confirm that the new chunk is actually registered and available.

The second approach is to extend an existing chunk rather than attach another one.

In GBase Database(GBase 8s), this operation uses the SQL Administration API.

First, mark the target chunk as extendable:

First: The argument is a chunk number, not a dbspace name.

Find the chunk number in the Chunks section of onstat -d. Use the actual chunk identifier shown in your instance rather than assuming that it matches the dbspace number.

Second: Check the underlying file system before extending.

The file system containing the chunk file must have enough available capacity. Otherwise, the extension cannot be completed successfully.

Unlike adding a new chunk, this approach does not create another chunk entry. It increases the capacity of an existing chunk.

Regardless of which method you choose, check three things before executing the expansion command.

Confirm that the target file system has enough available space for the requested expansion.

For an existing chunk extension, verify the chunk number through onstat -d. An incorrect chunk number means you may target the wrong storage resource.

When adding a new chunk, confirm that its page size matches the target dbspace.

Also check file ownership, permissions, and whether the target chunk path is already in use.

These checks are inexpensive compared with troubleshooting a failed storage change.

The two approaches solve the same capacity problem, but their operational characteristics differ.

My default preference is adding a new chunk, particularly in production environments.

It provides flexibility in storage allocation and can distribute capacity across different disks or file systems.

Extending an existing chunk is useful when the underlying file system has sufficient capacity and maintaining a stable chunk count is a priority.

However, neither approach should be treated as automatically risk-free or universally reversible. Check the supported rollback and recovery procedures for your GBase Database version before making production changes.

A successful expansion command is not the end of the operation.

There are three follow-up checks worth including in every GBase Database storage expansion runbook.

Verify the chunk count and available pages in the target dbspace.

Inspect the Chunks section and check the flags column.

Investigate any abnormal status, especially a chunk marked with PD.

This keeps the storage inventory consistent with the actual database configuration.

Without these checks, administrators may believe a dbspace has been expanded even though the expected capacity is not available.

GBase Database(GBase 8s) provides two practical ways to expand dbspace capacity:

The commands are not particularly complicated. The real work is checking prerequisites, selecting the right target, understanding the operational impact, and verifying the result.

A reliable storage expansion procedure should always follow this sequence:

Check capacity → Select expansion method → Execute → Verify chunk status → Update inventory

Online storage expansion is not complete when the command returns successfully. It is complete when the additional capacity is confirmed and the storage records reflect the actual state.

How does your team usually expand GBase Database dbspaces: adding new chunks or extending existing ones?

Keywords: GBase Database, GBase Database 8s, dbspace expansion, online storage expansion, add chunk, extend chunk, onspaces, SQL Administration API, onstat -d, database storage management

This card slots in just behind AMD's pricier RX 9070 in terms of raw performance, a gap that can be closed by Nvidia's superior DLSS upscaling quality and cleaner frame generation. The price is correspondingly lower too, as the red team's GPUs have steeply climbed in price lately.

Our review found that it's capable of playing most games at 1440p with high frame rates, at high detail levels with ray tracing. It'll also manage smooth 4K gameplay with raytracing across many titles, with the help of DLSS and DLSS FG.

As you can see from the handy chart above with our testing at 1080p, the standard RTX 5070 occupies pretty much the precise middle slot on the green team's lineup. If you flip through the album, you'll see we found that it's capable of playing most any game at 1440p and high framerates, at high detail levels with raytracing. It'll also manage 4K gameplay with raytracing across many titles, with the help of DLSS and DLSS FG, and with judicious tuning of the highest graphical settings that are usually diminishing returns anyway.

Not only that, prices for faster options are so far out there that their value propositions compared to this RTX 5070 range from iffy to laughable. Radeon RX 9070 XTs start at a grand, while the cheapest of Nvidia's RTX 5080 cards go for well over $2,200, with most models sitting around $2,500. As for the RTX 5090s, those are all speed-learning Mandarin and Cantonese and unavailable for comment. For now, this deal on a GeForce RTX 5070 12 GB is one of the best we've seen for the first day of Black Friday, but you'll need to pounce fast.

Draft a GitHub pull request in Claude Code with a /pr slash command

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you keep pasting the branch diff and "write a PR title and body, then wait" into Claude Code, turn that prompt into a slash command. The file name becomes the command; $ARGUMENTS lets you name the base branch or add extra instructions.

Use it as /pr, /pr main, or /pr develop include a migration note.

Slash commands are markdown files in .claude/commands. Claude Code injects the file as the prompt and replaces $ARGUMENTS with whatever you type after the command. Keeping "wait for confirm" and "never force-push or merge" in the command reduces unattended PR creation.

I keep copy-ready Claude Code configs (CLAUDE.md templates, slash commands, hooks, permission rules) in Vildanden Agent Config. There's a free sample: https://justly-mia-429.rehost.page/

What I learned building an audio-to-Suno-v6 prompt tool: language, regional genres and provider outages

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclosure: I'm the solo developer of music2prompt, the tool this post is about. This post is about what broke, not a sales pitch. Everything in it applies to any audio + LLM project. I drafted this post with help from an AI assistant; the numbers come from my own logs.

Suno v6 launched on September 9, 2026 and retired the older models. Overnight, a lot of people's prompts stopped working the way they used to. The most common request I see is simple: "I have a song I like. Write me the v6 prompt that sounds like it."

So I built a tool that listens to a reference track and writes two things: a Styles line and an Exclude Styles list. This is what I learned along the way.

My first version asked the audio model for everything: genre, mood, instruments, BPM. The genre and instruments were usually good. The BPM often wasn't. It was frequently off by double or half, or rounded to a "typical" tempo for the genre.

Now tempo is measured in the browser before anything is uploaded. In short:

The measured number goes to the model as a hint, and the UI marks it "measured" so the user knows it isn't a guess. The free BPM & key finder uses the same tempo code, plus chroma features and the classic Krumhansl key profiles for the key. One known weakness: relative keys get confused (A minor vs C major), so the key is shown as a suggestion, not a fact.

Lesson: if a property can be computed deterministically, compute it, and let the LLM do the parts that need judgment.

The biggest quality gap wasn't the genre. It was the language. If you prompt Suno with "Bollywood, tabla, strings" and leave out the language, you often get a song with an English accent. Users notice that immediately.

So the first field the model must return is the vocal language. "Instrumental" is a valid answer and is treated as one. For vocal tracks the prompt starts with sung in . Edge cases worth planning for:

A model can recognize a qawwali or an arabesk song. What it doesn't know is which words Suno actually responds to for that style. Native genre names are often too obscure, and generic words ("Indian music", "Middle Eastern") push Suno toward clichés.

What worked was a small hand-written regional genre → Suno tags glossary in the system prompt. Each entry has the genre name Suno understands, two or three signature instruments, and a rhythm or tempo anchor. For example:

The glossary is small and boring, and it was the most useful part of the system prompt. It also became the seed for a set of genre prompt pages I keep for 50 regional styles.

I wrote these rules as a small linter that runs in the browser. It's now a free prompt checker and a v5.5 → v6 converter. It's plain JavaScript with regexes and a few lookup tables, and it catches most mistakes before any AI call.

This is the part I wish I'd known earlier. One recent day, about a third of analyses failed. The logs showed:

The real fix was a second provider path. Analyses now go to Google's Gemini API directly first (a fast model with a 45 s limit, then a second model with a 25 s limit), and the old gateway is only a fallback. Since the switch, analyses take about 10–13 seconds instead of a median of 73. Lesson: a fallback chain only helps if the fallbacks don't share the same point of failure.

The most useful change wasn't a feature. It was one text column: error, holding a compact string like modelA:503 | modelB:429 || retry: modelA:500. Before it, all I had was "analysis failed". After it, I could see that most failures were whole-provider outages, not bad files. File size and failure rate turned out to be unrelated (r ≈ −0.06).

If you work with Suno and want to try it, the checker and the converter are free and run in your browser. I'm happy to answer questions about the tempo detection or the retry setup in the comments.

Apple may be developing its own AI agent that runs your Mac

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Apple appears to be working on its own AI agent to conduct computer work on behalf of users — including using apps, accessing websites, and coding, among other things.

The news was originally reported by Bloomberg, which spotted a job listing the iPhone maker recently posted for a senior engineering manager to oversee the creation of an “AI agent that can use a computer.”

“Imagine an AI agent that can use a computer the way a capable person does,” the posting says. “It browses the web, works across apps, runs commands, writes and debugs code, and calls services, and it chooses the right tool for each step. It does all this privately and with the polish people expect from Apple.”

The posting additionally notes that the agents should be able to move “fluidly between tools” and “carry out multi-step tasks across apps, browsers, command lines, code, and APIs.”

TechCrunch reached out to Apple for more information but did not receive a response.

Agents capable of computer work have become one of the hottest commodities in the tech industry over the past year. Major labs like Anthropic and OpenAI have launched such services, while other major tech brands — like Meta, which recently launched its Muse agent line — have also moved into the space. It makes sense that Apple — which is trying to expand its AI offerings in the new John Ternus era — would want to follow suit.

Bloomberg additionally notes that, at the same time that Apple is working on its own digital assistant, the company is making it significantly harder for third-party agents to gain total control over its computers.

The outlet notes that the version of its upcoming macOS 27.3 will deploy warnings discouraging users from granting full access to third-party agents. In October, Apple issued a notification to developers stating that the company planned to “introduce additional controls to ensure that users who genuinely wish to grant an app this extraordinary level of access can only do so with very explicit user action.”

Bloomberg writes that while the iPhone maker “has eyed introducing the service as soon as the end of next year,” progress on its in-house agent “remains in the early stages.”

Summer Walker’s Interscope Lawsuit Heats Up as Star Accuses Label of Misusing Her IP

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Amid an ongoing legal dustup over the terms of Walker's record deal, the singer is now alleging the label is improperly monetizing her content.

As Summer Walker and Interscope Records continue to hash out a contract dispute, the R&B singer is now accusing the Universal Music Group (UMG) label of wrongly monetizing her intellectual property.

Walker’s countersuit, submitted to Los Angeles court on Wednesday (Nov. 18) and reviewed by Billboard, comes a month after Interscope sued her for $50 million over the terms of their 2017 record deal. The document formalizes Walker’s assertion that the record deal is now void under California’s seven-year limit on personal services contracts.

Additionally, Walker is leveling up the dispute with new breach of contract and unjust enrichment claims. The singer alleges that Interscope has been using her intellectual property beyond the bounds of the record deal, which solely granted rights for the label to exploit and promote her music.

Walker alleges that she personally retains ownership of all her other IP, including the content she posts to social media. But according to Walker, Interscope has been reposting her digital content on YouTube and other platforms — and making money from it.

“UMG’s limited contractual rights to exploit basic works and use Cross-Complainant’s name, likeness and identity for authorized promotional purposes did not confer unrestricted ownership or monetization rights in the excluded digital assets,” reads the countersuit. “As a direct and proximate result of the foregoing conduct, UMG wrongfully obtained and retained revenues and other economic benefits attributable to property in which Cross-Complainant possessed superior ownership or possessory rights.”

Walker wants Interscope to give her the profits made from this content. She says the total amount would need to be established through evidence discovery, but the legal papers cite a damages figure of $20 million.

A lawyer for Walker, Keith Moten, told Billboard, “Summer has always approached her relationship with Interscope in good faith, and she remains appreciative of the role the label has played in her career. However, that relationship does not give Interscope unlimited rights over her creative work or the revenues generated from it.”

“Interscope has asserted its interpretation of the parties’ agreement, and Summer is equally entitled to challenge conduct that we believe exceeds the rights granted under that agreement,” added Moten. “Our cross-complaint seeks accountability for the alleged unauthorized exploitation of her creative assets and the revenues generated from it.”

A rep for Interscope did not immediately return a request for comment on the countersuit.

Walker has been locked in legal negotiations with Interscope since August, when she first sent a notice terminating their record deal under California’s seven-year statute. This state law, also known as Section 2855, allows individuals to cut off any personal contract that runs past that amount of time.

But the statute has a carveout specific to the music industry: Under a 1987 amendment, labels can sue for damages if an artist chooses to exit a record deal after seven years but before all of their album commitments have been fulfilled.

Interscope did exactly that in October. The label alleged that if Walker walks away, the singer will owe $50 million because she’s only delivered three projects from her contract’s five-album commitment: her 2019 debut Over It, which hit No. 2 on the Billboard 200, the 2021 chart-topper Still Over It and another No. 2 set, Finally Over It, in 2025.

Walker also has a separate ongoing legal action against the label/management firm LVRN, to which she jointly signed with Interscope in 2017. Walker claims the firm skimmed money from her music, touring and merch, while LVRN is countersuing over alleged unpaid commissions and royalties.

The S&P 500 Just Fell 4 Straight Weeks From a Record High. Here’s Why I’m Buying the Dip.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There's a big difference between a healthy pullback and the start of a bear market.

By David Dierking – Nov 19, 2026 at 7:50PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -2.02%) closed at a record 7,818.93 on Oct. 6. It finished that week higher, but from there it closed lower four weeks in a row, culminating in its post-election day drubbing.

As of now, the index is about 8% below its October high. The Nasdaq-100 and the tech sector are faring far worse, and investors are thinking about a potential bear market for the first time in over a year.

But if you go back several decades and look at situations like this -- the S&P 500 declining four straight weeks following a record high -- the outlook from here could actually be pretty bullish.

Since 1970, there have been 12 occasions when the S&P 500 has fallen four straight weeks right after hitting a new all-time closing high the previous week. Historically, the short term following these events has been more volatile. But it's the longer-term recovery period that's more meaningful.

Six months after the streak ended, the S&P 500 was higher in 10 of those 12 instances. The median gain was 13.4%, which is more than double the 5.2% median six-month gain during an average week since 1970.

Over the subsequent 12 months, the S&P 500 was higher on 11 of the 12 occasions, with a median gain of 16.5%.

I don't want to over-inflate the success rate here when the sample size is only 12, but I believe there's an explanation for this. Even bull markets have periods when stocks pull back. In many cases, it's not necessarily a sign that economic growth is stalling and a major bear market is imminent. It's just a normal part of the process of investing in risky assets. These are often normal market gyrations, and I think the data shows that over the long term, stocks tend to rise more often than fall.

Data source: Investing.com data and author's analysis of S&P 500 daily closing prices. "Streak ended" is the final trading day of the fourth losing week. Returns are measured from that close. Three months = 13 weeks; six months = 26 weeks; 12 months = 52 weeks.

One month from the streak's end, the chances of a positive return are pretty much a coin flip. It's not until three months out that, historically at least, the odds start to swing in your favor.

But I believe the overall trend is pretty clear and a good example of why a long-term buy-and-hold strategy is usually the best approach. In this scenario, the odds of generating a positive return have historically kept improving the longer you stay invested. That's a good framework to keep in mind whether stocks are steadily declining or near all-time highs.

History suggests that dips like these are much more likely to be healthy pullbacks during longer-term rising markets than a bear market warning.

Therefore, there's probably little need to make any major changes to your investment process. That means keeping your stock/bond allocations intact. Continue investing monthly or with every paycheck. Make sure your portfolio still aligns with your long-term goals.

If anything, you could consider positioning the equity segment of your portfolio a little more defensively. Moving from a core position in the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -1.98%) to something like the Vanguard Value ETF (VTV -1.58%) or the Invesco S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV -1.27%) could be a smart way to take some risk off the table while maintaining your equity exposure.

Market dips don't necessarily need to be feared. They can also be taken advantage of.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 790%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 202% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

Europe ready for talks with Russia on ending war in Ukraine, leaders say

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

European leaders have said they are ready for negotiations with Russia on ending the war in Ukraine, in a strong show of support for Kyiv.

"Europe has a clear and consistent and united negotiating position," UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham said, while German Chancellor Friedrich Merz stressed that Moscow's "maximalist demands are not realistic".

They were speaking at Berlin's summit of Ukraine's allies known as the Coalition of the Willing.

Donald Trump's son-in-law Jared Kushner, one of the two US envoys who were in Berlin, said the US president wanted to make a deal with Moscow "with Europe by his side". Russia's Vladimir Putin has so far rejected any European role in the US-led peace talks.

Trump had earlier held historic trilateral talks in Shenzhen, south-eastern China, with Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping and later dialled into the Berlin summit from Air Force One.

In a lengthy press conference following his talks with Trump and Xi, Putin addressed the question of multilateral peace talks, saying the Americans were "insistently" asking Russia to allow the Europeans to take part.

Putin claimed Russia was not against the idea but said Moscow wanted to clarify "what Europe will bring to these talks".

"That is why, through diplomatic channels, we have invited the Europeans to come to Moscow in order to determine their positions. And after that, we will decide in what format we will continue the dialogue with Ukraine."

But Putin also said that "every day" Russia heard that European leaders were talking about war coming in 2030. "But we are not planning to attack anyone... Is that normal? I do not think so," he said. Still, Putin added, Russia understood that "discussing issues linked to security in Europe is pointless without the Europeans".

Jared Kushner told reporters that the US president had come away from Shenzhen believing that "peace is achievable".

Striking an upbeat note, Kushner said that the Russians were willing to trust the instinct of the US envoys "on our behalf" that the Europeans should be at the table and that Moscow was now "comfortable" with Ukraine having security guarantees based on the idea enshrined in Nato's Article Five that requires allies to come to a country's defence if it comes under attack.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, who also was at the summit in Germany's capital, has long sought a guarantee equivalent to Nato's collective defence clause, and there has been little indication that Putin would accept that kind of condition.

Russia rejects outright the idea of Ukrainian membership of Nato, but reports say US negotiators have mulled the idea of giving Kyiv Article Five-style guarantees without being part of the Western defensive alliance if it agrees to painful territorial concessions.

Zelensky and the three European leaders â Merz, Burnham and French President Emmanuel Macron â were not as upbeat as Kushner, and the Ukrainian president made clear that the Russian president had spurned every proposal so far: "Putin has always said no."

The German chancellor was equally sceptical, although he said with US involvement the chance of peace was increasing. Europe was willing to enter "serious discussions on European security" with Russia, he explained, "as long as it's matched with a serious discussion and only first a peace agreement with Ukraine".

"These talks have to begin, and we will accept if conditions are acceptable for Europe and Ukraine," said Merz.

He also said any attempts to divide Europe into spheres of influence run by major powers was unacceptable, amid concerns on the continent that this issue may have been on the agenda at the trilateral talks in Shenzhen.

Analysis: Trilateral summit high on spectacle, short on substance

"A 'Yalta format' will not bring peace," Merz said, referring to the post-World War Two agreement between the US, the USSR and Britain in 1945.

He also sought to downplay the Shenzhen summit as above all "a photo opportunity."

"The US understands that there can be no peace without us. We, in turn, know that with the United States by our side, the prospects for peace increase," Merz added.

Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk also took exception to Trump's description of Putin as a "friend" at the Shenzhen summit, saying: "Mr Putin is not our friend and will never be."

Meanwhile, Macron urged Ukraine's allies to do the "maximum" to deliver the much-needed air defence systems Kyiv needed to repel deadly Russian attacks.

The French leader also urged the allies to "reopen the question" of Ukraine's membership in Nato.

There have been mixed messages from Moscow on European participation to the talks. Last week, following a visit to Moscow, the US envoys, Steve Witkoff and Kushner, appeared certain that Europe would soon have a seat at the negotiating table.

But the Russians were more circumspect and this week Putin's aide Yury Ushakov said a "great deal" would have to be done before the format could be expanded, suggesting that several more rounds of discussions would have to be held before any four-sided talks could take place.

Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.

Several buildings were damaged in the latest Russian attack on Ukraine's capital Kyiv

While Russia has engaged with several rounds of talks over the last year, little in Moscow's rhetoric or actions suggests that the Kremlin is considering scaling down its war on Ukraine.

It continues to pound Kyiv's energy infrastructure to cripple it as winter takes hold, increasing numbers of civilian casualties are being recorded across the country, and Kremlin officials have not budged from Russia's demands for Ukrainian sovereign territory.

As delegations were meeting in China and Germany on Thursday, several buildings in Kyiv's historic centre came under attack from drones. One person was killed in a strike on a residential building in the Podilskyi neighbourhood, while the Dutch and Austrian embassies as well as Saint Volodymyr's Cathedral reported damage.

And Putin also vowed that Moscow's response to a Ukrainian strike on the Crimean Bridge linking Russia with occupied Crimea would be "considerably more painful" than what Kyiv had achieved.

He also said he had discussed a "possible ceasefire in a number of areas" but had decided against it after the attack on the bridge.

Still, Putin claimed to be "interested" in ending the war and was ready to make compromises, while Ukraine and Europe were not.

"I assure you, we have no desire at all to continue this armed conflict," Putin said, despite the fact that Russia has in recent months intensified its attacks on Ukraine's critical infrastructure as cold winter approaches.

Kyiv and its European allies say that â in the absence of any major success on the vast front line â the Kremlin leader is seeking to break Ukrainian resistance by leaving millions of civilians without electricity and water supply.

Right now, you can pick up the Weber Q2200N Premium gas barbecue for AU$466.65 in the Amazon Black Friday sale, down from AU$549. That's a 15% saving of AU$82.35, although the offer only applies to the rather fetching Sky Blue colourway. It’s just one of a multitude of Weber Black Friday deals available at Amazon.

Don't let its compact dimensions fool you. The Q2200N has a generous 2,064cm² cooking area, which Weber claims is large enough to accommodate up to 12 burgers at once. And thanks to its high-domed lid and integrated thermometer, it's capable of roasting and baking as well as grilling.

If you're looking for a quality barbecue that won't dominate your balcony, courtyard or patio, the Weber Q2200N is a compelling option. And while 15% off isn't the biggest Black Friday discount I've seen, saving more than AU$80 on a premium Weber is certainly better than paying full price.

The changes "range from subtle to more significant"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Lucia and Jason's dynamic relationship is very much the heart and soul of GTA 6's story, offering a nuanced look at a complicated romance that changes based on your decisions in the game. Neglect your relationship and the lovers will drift apart, but nurture it and you'll see it blossom. It's no secret that GTA 6's take on Bonnie and Clyde can separate romantically based on player choice, and that they'll remain partners in crime no matter what, but it turns out pivotal story moments can change significantly based on your relationship status.

Talking to Game Informer, senior design director Luke Howard emphasizes the importance of Lucia and Jason having some sort of relationship with one another even if the romantic spark fizzles out. "This gave us a good platform on the main flow to expand particularly intense or emotional moments they experience with variation that can expand on their romantic relationship, while not losing anything if that doesn’t exist.

"In short, we did develop variation in scenes and interactions that range from subtle to more significant, but done in a way that should feel authentic to the player’s choices, relationship-wise and also criminal profile."

Howard uses an example of Lucia pulling off an intense armored truck robbery and Jason pulling her aside to check on her, which is a scene with "several variations where the performance direction was captured multiple ways to cover a range emotional energy between them.

"The actors deliveries are brilliant and nuanced – these small tender moments are where we tried to craft pieces that when added up together over the course of the story, author an authentic version of the player’s Jason and Lucia," says Howard.

I've only played a few hours of GTA 6 at this point and haven't had the chance to really change the course of Lucia and Jason's relationship, but I can already tell I'm going to become deeply invested in this part of the game thanks to strong writing and performances all around. Am I going to cry playing a GTA game? It sure seems so.

Bullish: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tokenizing corporate share registers could build a recurring revenue base that is less tied to volatile crypto trading volumes.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 19, 2026 at 7:44PM EST

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 18, 2026, Bullish (BLSH -1.97%) announced a $300 million share repurchase program and updated its financial outlook ahead of its pending Equiniti Group acquisition. The expansion into transfer agency services, which maintain official corporate shareholder registries, diversifies revenue beyond the volatility of digital asset trading cycles. Three takeaways from management show how the business is positioning for long-term growth.

Moving stock ownership onto public blockchains, where digital ledgers record share transfers instantly, requires direct participation from equity issuers rather than third-party entitlement tokens. By aiming to integrate tokenization directly into the share registers of roughly 3,000 public companies, the firm establishes a regulated foundation for digital share settlement.

Combining operations with Equiniti Group provides opportunities to eliminate redundant back-office expenses and automate share registry workflows using modern software. Management raised its expected net cost reductions over the medium term to range from $75 million to $100 million.

Navigating a broad downturn in digital asset trading volumes required management to tighten spending and reduce operational budgets. The board authorized an up to $300 million share repurchase program, demonstrating balance sheet flexibility to purchase undervalued stock while funding integration plans.

Depressed trading volumes drag near-term revenue, but digital share registration gains institutional traction. Integrating corporate share registries creates structural cost advantages over legacy operators.

Management is driving operational efficiency and expanding U.S. derivatives permissions. Unifying blockchain infrastructure with corporate registers sets up durable compounding.

Anthropic’s 7 founders cashed out nearly $2B in record-breaking IPO

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic became a public company on Thursday, raising a total of $81.3 billion including $1.95 billion for its seven founders. As we previously reported, each of them ponied up about 3.4 million shares apiece to sell in the IPO.

At the upgraded IPO price of $83 per share, that means they each pocketed about $280 million before underwriting fees and taxes. It looked like they had calculated the number of shares they planned to sell to give them each around $250 million if shares hit $74. That was the midpoint price from Anthropic’s original price range. So that’s a cool $30-ish million more for each of them than perhaps they were gunning for.

Anthropic implied in its IPO filings that the founders aren’t entirely in this for the money though. The company said that each of the founders has pledged to dedicate 80% of their personal equity in the company to philanthropy.

However, if any of them struggle to live on their quarter of a billion, they can scale back their charitable commitments. The company points out that these commitments are “voluntary, revocable expressions of each co-founder’s philanthropic intent and do not constitute legally binding obligations.”

With the IPO in the record books, there were a few other interesting numbers worth highlighting.

$104.8 billion. That’s really the amount Anthropic raised for its coffers (with a possibility of $117 billion, if the bankers buy all of their allotment of shares). In addition to selling its Class A common stock, it also sold a previously announced $26 billion worth of a special non-voting Class N stock to some of the biggest tech names in the industry.

Nvidia bought $10 billion of these Class N shares, Google bought $10 billion, and AMD, Broadcom, Micron, Microsoft, and TSMC divvied up the rest.

Just for comparison: When Facebook had its then record-breaking IPO in 2012, it raised $16 billion on IPO day.

28% stake to Amazon and Google combined. All told, at the higher IPO share price, Google wound up owning 13.4% of the company. Amazon also now has a big stake in Anthropic because when it previously invested in the AI lab as a startup, it was granted promissory notes that could be converted to equity. It converted a small percentage of them as part of the IPO, leaving it with a 14.6% stake. Had it converted all of them, Amazon could have owned nearly 24%.

The two large cloud providers now own 28% of Anthropic, the AI lab says.

More than $1 trillion. As mind-blowing as it is to raise nearly $105 billion, giving Anthropic nearly $230 billion of working capital available (along with capital it already had on hand), the company’s need for future capital is still far greater.

Anthropic needs about $70.7 billion just to cover its compute and infrastructure spending commitments in the next 12 months as of the quarter ended September 30, 2026. It needs $451.6 billion to cover these commitments before the end of 2030. And it has racked up hundreds of billions of other commitments that are either conditional (and may not happen) or haven’t been priced yet.

All told, the company has disclosed a ballpark of $1.05 trillion to $1.08 trillion in infrastructure commitments in its foreseeable future. This was a huge IPO. But it is a pricey business to run.

Michael Burry Says He Tried to Warn You About Nvidia, CoreWeave and Nebius. Here's What the 'Big Short' Investor Saw

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The famed investor took a victory lap as AI stocks slid again on Thursday.

By Jonathan Ponciano – Nov 19, 2026 at 7:43PM EST

Michael Burry, the investor made famous by "The Big Short," isn't shy about saying "I told you so."

"Coreweave, Nebius, NVIDIA, semis, I tried to warn you all," Burry posted on X on Thursday afternoon. "I didn't say short. But I provided the information one would need to sell."

He picked a good day for it. Nvidia (NVDA -3.30%) fell 3.3% on Thursday, CoreWeave (CRWV -6.11%) dropped 6.1%, and Nebius Group (NBIS -9.39%) sank 9.4%, as tech stocks gave up an early rally.

Here's what Burry saw, and what investors should do with it.

Michael Burry. Image source: Getty Images.

Burry's warnings came in pieces. In mid-October, he shared a Wall Street Journal story arguing that CoreWeave's sell-off showed the pressure from rising borrowing costs, adding his own commentary: "Dumb da dumb dumb da."

CoreWeave and Nebius are so-called neoclouds, companies that rent out Nvidia-powered computing to AI developers. Building their data centers takes enormous amounts of money, much of it borrowed, so higher interest rates hit them hard.

On Nvidia, his case is about the long run. He says he expects Nvidia's competitive edge to shrink over the next five years. In September, he also argued that its chips "may be economically depreciating waiting in warehouses while GAAP depreciation remains at zero," meaning the hardware could be losing value faster than the accounting shows.

The numbers have lent credence to his concern. CoreWeave closed Thursday at $44.91, down 76% from highs last year, and Nebius finished at $96.92, down 66% from its June peak. Nvidia has held up better but still sits 39% below its October high. The damage has spread across the chip industry, too: The VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH -2.62%), a basket of the biggest chip stocks, is down 33% from its June high.

As he laid out his long-term worries about Nvidia earlier this month, Burry said he imagined "the whole market is going to think this is a heck of a trade fairly soon." But since then, the stock has fallen another 16%.

Nvidia's business, meanwhile, hasn't cracked. Its revenue jumped 103% to $115.8 billion last quarter, and management raised its growth forecast for next year. The stock's slide this month coincided with Jensen Huang's sudden resignation as CEO, rather than a drop in sales.

If you own neocloud stocks like CoreWeave and Nebius, watch how much they borrow and what that debt costs them. If you own Nvidia, watch whether customers keep buying at today's pace, and whether the chips they buy hold their value.

The AI stocks that come out of this sell-off in good shape will be the ones with the best answers to those questions. For long-term investors, that's a better guide than any single post on X, even one from Michael Burry.

LSD changes the brain’s rhythm in a way earlier studies missed

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Brain recordings from 17 volunteers show that LSD speeds up two brain rhythms even as it weakens them.

Brain recordings of people on psychedelics have told the same story for years: the brain’s steady electrical rhythms get weaker, as if the drug were turning the volume down.

A new analysis of recordings from 17 people given LSD finds that the volume isn’t the whole story. The drug also turns up the tempo.

LSD pushes two of the brain’s main rhythms to faster beats, and it makes the brain’s electrical activity more detailed and less predictable.

Venkatesh Subramani, a doctoral student at the Université de Montréal, led the analysis with his supervisor Karim Jerbi. Jerbi is a psychology professor there and a researcher at Mila, the Quebec artificial intelligence institute.

Co-authors included Nicolas Farrugia and Giulia Lioi of IMT Atlantique in France and Robin Carhart-Harris of the University of California, San Francisco.

“Imagine a radio station switching its broadcast frequency from 99.5 to 100.5 FM,” Jerbi said.

“If your radio stays tuned to 99.5, the signal may seem distorted or lost. In the same way, overlooking the shift to faster brain rhythms can lead us to misinterpret LSD’s effects on the brain.”

Groups of neurons firing together produce rhythms that a sensor outside the head can pick up. Researchers call them brain waves.

Alpha waves dominate when you rest with your eyes closed. Beta waves are faster and show up during concentration and decision-making.

Under psychedelics those waves look weaker, and the standard reading is that neurons are firing less in unison. Subramani’s team pointed out a catch.

If a rhythm speeds up, its peak can shift outside the frequency range researchers are measuring. That shift can make the wave look weaker even if its strength hasn’t changed.

So the team pulled the two possibilities apart. They first stripped out the brain’s rhythm-free background activity.

Then they located each person’s actual alpha and beta peaks under LSD and under placebo, and measured the power at those peaks.

The recordings were made at Imperial College London by Carhart-Harris’s group. Seventeen healthy volunteers came in twice, two weeks apart.

In one session, they received an intravenous injection of 75 micrograms of LSD, roughly the weight of a grain of salt. In the other they received a saline placebo, without being told which was which.

About four hours after the injection, each person lay in a magnetoencephalography scanner. It records the faint magnetic fields that neurons produce, with timing down to the millisecond.

They rested with their eyes closed for seven minutes, and in a separate seven-minute block they listened to ambient music.

The Montreal team reanalyzed those recordings with newer signal-processing tools and a machine-learning model trained to tell the LSD sessions from the placebo sessions.

Under LSD, alpha waves ran about 2 cycles per second faster than on placebo, and beta waves about 5 cycles per second faster, across large areas of the cortex. The waves did also weaken.

Once the speed-up was accounted for, that weakening held up as real rather than an artifact of looking in the wrong place.

The two effects didn’t fully overlap. The weakening was strongest in regions that handle hearing, language, and emotion.

The speed-up followed its own map, and the regions that weakened most tended to speed up least.

Anesthetics do the opposite to the alpha rhythm, slowing it down as the cortex becomes less excitable. The authors read the faster alpha under LSD as a sign of a more excitable cortex.

They also noted that psilocybin and DMT haven’t been shown to shift the peaks this way, so the speed-up may be a signature specific to LSD.

A fractal is a shape that repeats itself in its own parts, the way each floret of a Romanesco broccoli is a smaller broccoli.

The team measured how fractal the brain signal was over time, and how varied its patterns were. Both rose under LSD.

The increases were largest in areas tied to vision, hearing, memory, and mental imagery, and smallest in the motor cortex.

That split fits a familiar feature of the drug. Perception and inner experience change a great deal, while people can still move normally.

People on LSD often report seeing repeating geometric patterns with their eyes closed. The paper links the two only loosely.

“Our findings lend support to this intriguing hypothesis, but they do not yet confirm it,” Jerbi said. “We measure the fractal structure of brain signals over time, not the fractal structure of the images people perceive.”

Given only the shift in peak frequencies, the machine-learning model identified the LSD session more than 82% of the time.

Models trained on the fractal and complexity measures did almost as well. The brain regions the models relied on matched the ones the conventional statistics had flagged.

“Our machine-learning algorithm can distinguish brain states under LSD from those under placebo using brain signals alone,” Subramani said.

“By examining the clues it relies on, we can identify LSD’s signatures in brain activity.”

Music, which is routinely played during psychedelic therapy, didn’t amplify any of these effects. If anything the trend ran the other way, with slightly smaller brain changes during the music block.

That difference didn’t survive the statistical correction, and the authors called it exploratory.

Seventeen people is a small group, and everyone received a single dose while healthy, so the study says nothing about whether any of this helps patients.

The volunteers rated their experience at the peak of the drug’s effect, while the scanner ran about two and a half hours later.

The authors wrote that this gap may be why brain changes and reported experiences lined up only loosely.

The recordings also included no direct measure of muscle activity, which can contaminate the fastest brain signals. So the authors treated their high-frequency results with extra caution.

The next question is whether the speed-up really belongs to LSD alone.

Subramani and his colleagues are now running the same analysis on recordings taken under psilocybin, the active ingredient in magic mushrooms, and under DMT.

The aim is to see whether the three drugs produce the same signatures in the brain or different ones.

The full study was published in the journal Communications Biology.

Ledger’s crypto wallet implant scare spreads to Berlin as Dubai cases come under investigation

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ledger offers X-ray inspections for suspect wallets as its hardware tampering investigation expands to Berlin, Dubai and Southeast Asia.

Ledger is offering limited-time X-ray inspections for suspect hardware wallets as its tampering investigation reaches Berlin.

In a Nov. 18 update, the wallet maker said its support team can arrange for customers to send a device to its factory in Vierzon, France, for inspection and return.

The offer lets owners have their device examined for physical alterations. For anyone who has already used a suspect device, Ledger advised moving funds to a wallet with a fresh recovery phrase. The company also said in its advisory that the hardware it found was designed to capture those words during setup.

Ledger noted that the German cases it had investigated were localized to Berlin. The company advised concerned customers who bought a Ledger wallet from a third party there from January 2026 onward to contact its support team.

It also asks concerned buyers who purchased from third parties in Southeast Asia or Dubai to get in touch.

Ledger revealed it is still investigating reports involving two devices in Dubai and is working with distribution partners to learn how they entered circulation. It reported one affected Nano Gen 5 as it continues its investigation into Southeast Asian resellers.

The advisory described the inspection as an option for devices that raise concerns, so customers can ask Ledger Support to arrange secure shipment through an official ticket.

Ledger recommended sending a device only through that channel, and that support will never ask for a recovery phrase. Its guidance for buyers in Berlin, Dubai, and Southeast Asia describes when to contact Ledger, while support handles the arrangements for individual devices sent to the factory.

The inspection can look inside the submitted device for added hardware, since Ledger’s Genuine Check verifies the secure chip's authenticity. Where that chip remains intact, the check can miss a physical addition.

Ledger reported that all the devices it confirmed in this incident carried the same physical modification and that the investigation found no flaw in its secure chip or operating system.

In its Oct. 15 disclosure, Ledger said genuine wallets had been altered after leaving its control and distributed through Southeast Asian resellers.

The company said the samples it examined from stores, warehouses, and its own stock at the time were clean. In November, it described the Berlin findings as part of the same incident and that it still had no confirmed case involving a device bought directly from ledger.com.

Ledger advised owners of an unused suspect device to leave it unopened, keep it out of use, and contact official support. They should avoid using it to set up a new wallet or restore an existing one.

For owners who have already used a suspect device, Ledger advised moving funds to a new wallet with a fresh recovery phrase, generated on a device bought directly from its website. It also recommended avoiding reusing any passphrase used on the suspect device.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Ledger’s Nov. 18 advisory gives owners different advice depending on whether they’ve used the device.

A factory scan checks the hardware. Moving funds to a new wallet puts them under a fresh recovery phrase.

Ledger’s advisory gives no closing date, turnaround or eligible-model list.

The two branches summarize the owner precautions in Ledger’s Nov. 18 advisory. The inspection strip shows the separate physical check.

Ledger’s advisory gives no closing date, turnaround or eligible-model list.

The recovery phrase is the set of words that lets an owner restore access to a wallet on a compatible device. Entering the same words on replacement hardware brings up the same wallet and leaves the original recovery credential in use.

Someone who already obtained the credentials needed to restore that wallet could use a compatible device of their own. Moving funds to a wallet with a fresh phrase changes the credentials that control those funds.

Meanwhile, an X-ray can look for evidence of physical tampering. Finding an implant would show the unit was altered.

Whether anyone already obtained a particular owner’s recovery words is a separate question that the scan alone cannot answer.
