Claude Jira: Built for Tickets, Slow at Team Metrics

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 19 • Originally published at infograph.ai

This was first published on the Infograph blog: https://infograph.ai/blog/claude-jira

Connect Claude to Jira with Atlassian's own connector and it can find tickets, read them, write new ones, comment, and move them across the board, all with your Jira permissions. Atlassian built those tools around single tickets, so work on one ticket or one epic is what this setup does best. It's one connector in Claude, or one command in Claude Code, Anthropic's coding tool (setup below).

Team numbers are harder. Take cycle time: how long a ticket takes from the day someone starts it to the day it's done. Every ticket shows the day it was created, but the day someone started it is only in the ticket's history, its log of every change. So "What was our median cycle time last quarter?" needs the history of every ticket you finished, and Atlassian's history tool reads one ticket at a time.

The shortcut Claude can take is to count from the day each ticket was created to the day it was resolved. That's lead time, which includes the weeks a ticket sat in the backlog, so it's usually a bigger number.

So use Claude for the ticket in front of you, and a dashboard built on every ticket's history for the team's numbers.

Most of what a lead asks of Jira falls into two piles. One is about a ticket or an epic: what's blocking PAY-388, or write these notes up as a story. The other is about the team: where do our tickets wait, and are we getting faster?

Claude with Atlassian's connector fits the first pile. It works where you already are, in a chat or in Claude Code, and it writes and moves tickets as well as reading them. One ticket or one epic takes a few tool calls (each call is one action, like fetching a ticket).

The second pile is where it slows down. A question about many tickets' histories takes a call per ticket. Claude also works each answer out from scratch, so it may pick different dates or tickets from one week to the next. And because it can write, it can move the wrong ticket as easily as the right one.

Infograph, which is what we make, fits the second pile. Our Jira connection keeps every status change on every ticket, so cycle time and time in status are counted across all of them, the same way each week. You get a dashboard: a board the team can look at in standup, and a link you can send to leadership.

Its limits: it can't change Jira, so it won't write or move a single ticket. Its numbers are only as fresh as the last sync, and it needs a one-time setup, including which of your statuses mean waiting.

You can also have both: Claude can ask Infograph's synced Jira history directly (how, below).

Atlassian makes the connector. In Claude's directory it's Atlassian MCP, marked Anthropic verified, and it covers Jira, Confluence and other Atlassian apps. MCP (Model Context Protocol) is the standard that lets an AI app like Claude plug into other software. Atlassian runs the server, so there's nothing to install: it hands Claude a set of tools, each a single action such as "get this ticket" or "move it to Done". It works with Jira Cloud, the version Atlassian hosts for you.

On a Team or Enterprise plan, your Owner (Claude's account admin) adds Atlassian for the company first: Organization settings, then Connectors, then Browse connectors, then Add to your team (Anthropic). Then everyone connects their own account:

In Claude on the web or the desktop app, open Customize, then Connectors, and press +. Find Atlassian MCP and press Connect (or Install).

Follow the prompts to sign in with the Atlassian account you use for Jira. Claude then gets your existing permissions and nothing more, Atlassian says. The first connection at a company may need your Atlassian admin to approve it (Atlassian).

In a new chat, press +, choose Connectors and check Atlassian is switched on. Then ask for something small, like the open tickets in one project.

In Claude Code, Atlassian's command adds the server, and /mcp inside a session signs you in:

Plenty of guides still give a /v1/ address. If you used one, remove that connector and add Atlassian MCP again from the directory, or in Claude Code run claude mcp remove atlassian and the command above. Atlassian moves anything left on v1 to the v2 tools on 1 March 2027 (Atlassian).

Claude acts with your own Jira permissions, so it can create, edit and move whatever you can. Its tools for deleting tickets and changing projects stay off until your Atlassian admin turns them on. That admin can also stop it writing to Jira at all, in Atlassian Administration under Connectors, then Atlassian MCP server, then Permissions, then Edit details (Atlassian).

In a chat, Claude asks for your approval before it uses a tool. Only choose Allow always for tools that just read, like a search or fetching a ticket. Research, Claude's long multi-step mode, is the exception: it uses connector tools without asking, so Anthropic advises switching off the tools that write before you use it (Anthropic).

If you came here for Claude writing the code behind a ticket, that's a different product: Claude Agent for Jira. You assign it a ticket and it proposes the code change on GitHub. Atlassian says it needs Jira's Standard, Premium or Enterprise plan with Rovo, Atlassian's AI, switched on.

Each of these touches one ticket, one epic or one search, which is what Atlassian's tools are built around. Name the project (and the Jira site, if you use more than one) so the search stays small, and ask for a draft before anything is created or changed.

Monday, the bugs that came in over the weekend:

After planning, the notes nobody wants to type up:

Projects often have required fields of their own. Atlassian's server has a tool that reads which fields each issue type needs, so ask Claude to check them before it drafts.

Midweek, when someone asks how the checkout epic is going:

In Jira terms, cycle time runs from In Progress to Done, and lead time from the day a ticket is created. Our guide to time in status in Jira covers the difference, and Jira's own reports and the other ways to get both.

Here's what the median for last quarter asks of the tools Atlassian documents:

Atlassian changes this list often, so check its supported tools page before you build a habit on it.

What each question asks of Atlassian's Jira tools, drawn from its supported tools page.

If your team finished 200 tickets last quarter, that's 200 history reads, plus extra pages for long histories, before any arithmetic starts. That's slow going for one chat, which makes the shortcut tempting: every ticket carries its created and resolved dates on the ticket itself, with no history to read. If Claude counts from those, the number is lead time, whatever the answer calls it.

Atlassian's own skills for Claude, the written instructions Claude follows for a job, avoid reading many histories too. The release check reads the history of at most two blocked tickets, to date when each became blocked, because "only a changelog gives that reliably". The sprint dashboard skill judges how long unfinished work has sat by each ticket's last update instead.

You can still ask. Just ask how it got there, too: which dates it used, and how many tickets' histories it read. Try one sprint before a whole quarter, and check two tickets by hand in their History tab. In Claude Code, an engineer can have Claude write a script against Jira's REST API that pulls every history and does the sums. That's a fair route, and it leaves you with one more script someone owns and reruns each week.

A team number needs every status change for every ticket, kept in one place and counted the same way each week. That's what our Jira connection is for. You sign in with Atlassian once, and the first sync brings every ticket anyone has touched in the last two years, each with its full history. From then on a question about the quarter is one query over all of it, and the answer is a dashboard you can come back to.

We built one on sample data for Rotaloom, a made-up software company with three Jira projects. The Jira dashboard showcase walks through it, and the dashboard itself opens without signing in. Here is the same question over the last 90 days:

Sample data. Each dot is one finished ticket, timed from its own history: half of the 287 took under 8.1 days, and the slowest 43, about one in seven, took longer than 16.0 days.

Each dot runs from the ticket's first move into In Progress to its last move to Done. Through Atlassian's tools that would be 287 history reads; here it's one query over history that's already synced.

To build your own, connect Jira in Infograph. The guide covers each step and the errors you might see, such as a site that isn't in the list or an app your admin has blocked.

Then ask Infograph for it. In Jira, a status like In Review counts as In Progress, even though the ticket is waiting for someone. So change the waiting statuses in the prompt to the ones your team uses.

You can add any of these to the same question:

One Jira connection fits Infograph's Free plan, where you sync by pressing Sync. On the Plus plan it syncs daily, and on Pro every 30 minutes (see the plans). Infograph reads with your Jira permissions and can't change anything in Jira.

To ask from Claude instead, add Infograph as a custom connector beside Atlassian's:

In Claude, open Customize, then Connectors, and press + Add, then Add custom connector. Name it Infograph and paste https://app.infograph.ai/mcp.

Press Continue, keep the suggested sign-in settings, press Continue again, then Add, and sign in to Infograph.

On a Team or Enterprise plan, your Owner adds it first, in Organization settings, then Connectors (Anthropic). Claude's Free plan allows one custom connector.

In Claude Code, it's one command, then /mcp to sign in:

Your Jira site has to be connected in Infograph first. After that, Claude can ask the synced history directly: Infograph runs the question over every ticket and sends back just the answer. Keep Atlassian's connector for the ticket work; the two sit side by side.

A rule of thumb for the two: if the question has a ticket key in it, ask Claude with Atlassian's connector. If it has a date range in it, ask the synced history.

Yes. Atlassian MCP, built by Atlassian, is in Claude's connector directory and marked Anthropic verified. It covers Jira, Confluence and other Atlassian apps, and works with your existing Atlassian permissions.

https://mcp.atlassian.com/v2/mcp. Older guides give a v1 address. Atlassian says that from 1 March 2027 anything still on v1 starts getting the v2 tools; in Claude, replace an old v1 connector with the one in the directory, and in Claude Code sign in again.

Atlassian's troubleshooting page says an expired sign-in fails silently, so disconnect and reconnect it in Customize, then Connectors. A first connection at a company may need your Atlassian admin to approve it. And if your company only lets certain IP addresses reach Atlassian, Atlassian notes that calls from a hosted AI app can come from the AI provider's addresses rather than your VPN, so an admin may need to allow them.

On your own plan, read each approval and only choose Allow always for tools that just read.

It can, by reading each ticket's history. Through Atlassian's documented tools that's a history read per ticket, or an engineer can have Claude Code script it against Jira's REST API. Either way, ask which dates it used: created to resolved is lead time, not cycle time.

Atlassian publishes skills for Claude, written instructions it follows for a job, in its atlassian-mcp-server repository on GitHub: triage-issue, capture-tasks-from-meeting-notes, generate-status-report, assess-release and more. Atlassian lists them as one of its ways to connect Claude to Jira. Read any skill before you add it, since it can tell Claude to use every tool it has.

Anthropic says connectors are available to all Claude users. On Atlassian's side, only tools marked Enriched on its supported tools page use Rovo credits, Atlassian's allowance for its AI features, and the Jira tools in this post aren't marked that way.

Atlassian lists an Atlassian Cloud site as the requirement for its MCP server. Infograph's Jira connection is also for Jira Cloud only.

What Is LLM Throughput? Tokens per Second, Concurrency, and Batching

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When teams evaluate how a large language model (LLM) will perform in production, two families of metrics come up repeatedly. One family describes latency: how quickly an individual request is served. The other describes throughput: how much total work the system can accomplish in a given period. Throughput is what determines whether a deployment can serve ten users or ten thousand, and it is the metric most directly tied to infrastructure cost per request. Yet it is frequently misunderstood, partly because the headline figure, "tokens per second," can mean several different things depending on whether you are counting one stream or the whole system.

This article explains what LLM throughput actually measures, why it depends so heavily on concurrency and batching, and how it trades off against latency. The goal is a clear and accurate mental model, grounded in how inference hardware behaves.

Throughput is the rate at which a system completes work. For LLM inference, "work" is almost always measured in tokens, the sub-word units the model reads and generates. The most common throughput metric is tokens per second, but it is critical to distinguish two senses of it:

These two numbers can move in opposite directions, which is the single most important and counterintuitive fact about LLM serving. Pushing more requests through the system at once usually raises aggregate throughput while lowering each individual stream's rate. Confusing the two leads to bad capacity decisions, so it is always worth asking which one a given "tokens per second" figure refers to.

Throughput is also sometimes expressed in requests per second, which is useful when requests have similar shapes, but tokens per second is more robust because LLM requests vary enormously in input and output length. A request that generates 10 tokens and one that generates 1,000 tokens are very different amounts of work even though each counts as a single request.

To understand why concurrency raises aggregate throughput, you have to look at what the hardware is doing during generation. Modern accelerators are massively parallel, with enormous arithmetic capacity. During the token-by-token decoding phase of generation, however, the dominant cost is not arithmetic. It is reading the model's weights from memory. For every single token a model generates, it must stream its full set of parameters (or the relevant portions) out of memory and through the compute units.

If the server generates a token for just one request, it reads all those weights to produce a single token. The expensive memory read is "amortized" over one unit of useful work, and most of the chip's arithmetic capacity sits idle. This is deeply inefficient.

Batching fixes this. By grouping many requests together and generating their next tokens in one pass, the server reads the model weights once and uses them to produce tokens for all requests in the batch. The costly memory read is shared across many units of work. As a result, aggregate throughput climbs almost linearly with batch size, up to the point where the hardware's arithmetic capacity or memory for the KV cache becomes the bottleneck.

Concurrency is the number of requests a system is actively processing at the same time. Higher concurrency enables larger batches, which drives aggregate throughput up. But concurrency is not free, and it cannot grow without bound. Two resources constrain it.

The first is memory for the KV cache. Each in-flight request maintains a cache of key and value tensors representing the context it has processed so far. This cache grows with the sequence length and consumes accelerator memory alongside the model weights. The more concurrent requests, and the longer their contexts, the more memory the KV cache demands. When memory runs out, the system can no longer admit new requests, must queue them, or in some designs must evict and recompute context, all of which hurt performance.

The second is compute saturation. As batch size grows, the decoding step eventually stops being purely memory-bound and starts being limited by arithmetic throughput. Beyond that point, adding more concurrent requests no longer increases aggregate throughput; it only increases per-request latency, because each step now takes longer. The system has reached its efficient operating ceiling.

The art of serving LLMs efficiently is largely about operating near the "knee" of this curve: enough concurrency to keep the hardware well-utilized and aggregate throughput high, but not so much that latency becomes unacceptable or memory overflows.

Early batching approaches were "static": the server collected a batch, ran it to completion, and only then started the next batch. This is wasteful because requests in a batch finish at different times. A request generating a short answer finishes early but its slot stays occupied, idle, until the longest request in the batch completes.

Continuous batching (also called in-flight or dynamic batching) solves this. Instead of waiting for the whole batch to finish, the scheduler works at the granularity of individual decode steps. As soon as any request completes and frees its slot, a waiting request is admitted into the batch immediately. The batch is continuously topped up, keeping utilization high and reducing the time new requests spend queuing. Continuous batching is one of the most significant throughput improvements in modern inference servers, often increasing effective throughput several-fold over static batching for mixed workloads.

A simple back-of-the-envelope relationship connects these quantities. If you know your target per-stream rate and your aggregate capacity, you can estimate how many users you can serve concurrently:

Throughput and concurrency relationships aggregate_tps = 2000 # system tokens/sec at the chosen batch size per_stream_tps = 40 # tokens/sec each active request should receive How many streams can run at the target per-stream rate? max_concurrency = aggregate_tps / per_stream_tps = 2000 / 40 = 50 concurrent streams Throughput for a workload, given average output length and request rate avg_output_tokens = 300 requests_per_sec = aggregate_tps / avg_output_tokens = 2000 / 300 ~= 6.7 requests/sec sustained

These are approximations, not guarantees. Real aggregate throughput depends on prompt lengths (long prompts spend more time in the compute-bound prefill phase), output lengths, how uniform the requests are, and memory headroom. But the structure of the calculation is sound and useful for capacity planning: aggregate throughput divided by the per-stream rate you are willing to deliver gives you a rough concurrency ceiling.

The central tension in LLM serving is that throughput and latency pull against each other. Driving the system toward maximum aggregate throughput means running large batches at high concurrency, which lengthens each individual request's time per token and can increase queuing delay before a request even starts. Conversely, optimizing for the lowest possible latency for each user means running small batches, which leaves hardware underutilized and raises the cost per token.

There is no universally correct setting. A batch inference job that processes a million documents overnight should be tuned for maximum throughput, since no human is waiting on any individual result. An interactive assistant should cap batch sizes and concurrency to protect per-user responsiveness, accepting a higher cost per token. Most production systems expose configuration, such as maximum batch size, maximum concurrency, and queue limits, precisely so operators can position the system along this trade-off curve for their use case.

In a deployment serving multiple applications or models, throughput and concurrency are managed at the layer where traffic is proxied, at a gateway that sits in front of the model servers. Concentrating traffic management there allows concurrency limits, queuing, and load balancing across model replicas to be enforced consistently, so that one bursty client cannot starve others and aggregate capacity is used efficiently. This separation of concerns, with the model server focused on efficient batched generation and the routing layer focused on distributing and limiting traffic, is a common and effective pattern.

Keeping n8n maintainable as a small team: sub-workflows, naming and a few house rules

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The first ten n8n workflows are fun. Around workflow thirty, things change: nobody remembers which "Webhook 3" handles which form, the same Slack-notification block has been copy-pasted into twelve places, and changing one API key means opening half the instance. None of this is n8n's fault. It's what happens to any automation setup that grows without a few conventions.

These are the house rules I set up for small teams running self-hosted n8n. None of them need extra tools.

A consistent pattern makes the workflow list searchable:

The [LIB] prefix marks reusable sub-workflows (more on those below). Tags work too, but a name prefix is visible everywhere, including execution logs.

HTTP Request1 and IF2 mean nothing three months later. Rename nodes to say what they do: Get open invoices, Has email?, Format reminder text. When an execution fails, the error points straight at a readable step.

If the same group of nodes appears in more than two workflows, it should probably be its own workflow, called with the Execute Workflow node. Typical candidates:

Give the sub-workflow an Execute Workflow Trigger with a clear input shape, and document it in a sticky note:

Now when you switch alert channels or change message formatting, you change one workflow instead of twelve.

Avoid five slightly different credentials for the same Google account. Name credentials by system and purpose, like Google Sheets - finance (service account), and reuse them. When a token rotates, there's one place to update.

Hard-coded IDs scattered through nodes (sheet IDs, chat IDs, folder IDs) are a common source of "it worked in testing". Options that work well on a small setup:

Set an Error Workflow in each production workflow's settings and point it to one shared handler that sends the workflow name, the failing node and the execution URL to your alert sub-workflow. A silent failure is the most expensive kind.

A short sticky note at the start of each workflow answering three questions saves a lot of time:

n8n workflows are JSON. A nightly job that exports them via the n8n API or CLI and commits them to a private repo gives you history, diffs and a backup for free. It also makes reviewing changes possible when more than one person edits automations.

Even a second, small n8n instance (or at least a [TEST] prefix and test credentials) keeps experiments from sending real emails to real customers.

None of these rules is complicated, and you don't need all of them on day one. Naming, error workflows and one or two shared sub-workflows already make a big difference. I've written more about moving teams onto self-hosted n8n in this Zapier-to-n8n migration guide, and I set up and maintain these systems for small businesses through my n8n automation service.

How we generate endless, always-solvable pipe puzzles in under 60 lines of vanilla JS

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Lantern Links is a pipe puzzle. A glowing core sits near the middle of a grid, every other tile holds a piece of pipe, and you rotate tiles until light reaches every lantern. We wanted levels that never run out, never need a hand-made level file, and are never unsolvable. This post walks through the generator, which is about 60 lines of plain JavaScript inside a single 71 KB index.html with no libraries.

A short version of the idea is in a devlog on itch. Here we go deeper into the parts that are easy to get wrong: how the tree is grown, what the seed covers, how par is counted, and how the board shape is chosen.

Most "shuffle until solvable" approaches generate a random board and then check it. We do the opposite. We build a finished, fully lit board first, and then scramble it. If you start from a solution and only apply moves that the player can undo, the puzzle is solvable by construction. No solver is needed.

A spanning tree of the grid graph has exactly these properties. It reaches every cell, it uses N-1 edges for N cells, and a connected graph with N-1 edges cannot contain a cycle. So the generator is a random spanning tree algorithm.

Each tile is a 4-bit mask, one bit per side:

Rotating a tile clockwise moves each bit one place left, and the top bit wraps around to the bottom. That is the whole rotation function. The kind of piece falls out of the bit count: one bit is a lantern (a dead end), two opposite bits are a straight, two adjacent bits are a bend, three bits are a junction. There is no separate piece type anywhere in the code.

Carving an edge between two neighbours sets one bit on each tile: the direction bit on the tile we leave, and the opposite bit on the tile we enter. Here is the generator, trimmed only of its state handling:

A plain randomised depth-first search always continues from the newest cell. That makes long, snaking corridors with few branches. Picking a random cell from the frontier instead (the idea behind a randomised Prim's algorithm) gives short, bushy trees with lots of junctions. Neither feels right for this game: the first is too easy to read, the second looks like a mess of crosses.

The line that sets pickIdx mixes the two. With probability 0.45 we extend the newest cell (depth-first behaviour), and otherwise we extend a random cell from the stack. This is usually called the growing tree algorithm. The 0.45 is a single constant. Raising it pushes the look of the levels toward corridors, and lowering it toward bushes. Our README describes the generator as a randomised depth-first search, which is accurate for the 45 percent case only, so we are correcting that here.

A cell leaves the stack when it has no unvisited neighbours left. The loop ends when the stack is empty, and because the grid is connected, every cell has been visited by then.

(X is the core, o is a tile, dashes and bars are carved edges. A cycle would need an edge to a cell that is already in the tree, and the generator never does that.)

Once the tree exists, lanterns need no extra step. A tile with exactly one connection is a leaf, and a leaf is a lantern, unless it is the core:

We build the list of lanterns once per level from the solution mask. That is why the lantern count differs from level to level and from seed to seed: it is whatever the tree happens to produce.

To turn the answer into a puzzle, each tile gets a random number of clockwise quarter turns, and par is the sum of the taps needed to undo them:

First, tapsTo returns the fewest clockwise taps that bring a tile to its target mask, which is 0 to 3. A straight pipe repeats after two turns, so for a straight it is never more than 1; a junction or bend can need up to 3.

Second, there is a guard for the unlucky case where the random rotations leave the board already solved. On a 16-tile board that is not impossible. We turn the first tile that actually changes when rotated.

Third, par is measured against the board we generated, not against a proof of the minimum. We have not shown that every board has only one solved arrangement, so a player who finds a different valid arrangement might beat par. In practice par is a good yardstick, and stars are cheap: three stars at or under par, two up to par plus half of par (rounded up), one otherwise.

Math.random() cannot be seeded, so we use mulberry32, a small 32-bit generator:

The seed is lv * 7919 + 17, so the same level number grows the same tree. The same stream carries on into scramble, so the starting orientation is also the same. There is one catch, and it matters for the next section: the tree depends on the board's columns and rows as well as the seed. Level 12 on a 7 by 8 board is the same puzzle for everyone, but level 12 on a 6 by 10 board is a different one.

Restart is a small exception. It keeps the tree and re-scrambles with a time-based seed, so you get the same network with fresh orientations.

The old version of the game used a square board: 4 by 4 at level 1, growing one row and column every three levels, up to 10 by 10. On a phone held upright, a square board leaves most of the screen empty. So the board shape is now chosen at the start of each level from the free space:

The tile count is kept between k squared and 1.5 times k squared, so difficulty still grows with the level. Inside that range we try every columns-by-rows pair and score it by fill: the share of the free area covered by square tiles, where the tile side t is the smaller of the width and height limits. A new candidate wins if it fills clearly more space; if two shapes are within half a percent, the one with fewer tiles wins. The result is, for example, a tall 4 by 6 board on a phone and a wide 5 by 4 on a laptop.

One more rule keeps this from being annoying. While a level is untouched, resizing the window or rotating the phone re-runs chooseShape and rebuilds the level. After your first move the shape is frozen and the board only rescales. Otherwise the puzzle would change under your fingers.

Win detection is a breadth-first walk from the core. A neighbour joins the lit set only if both tiles have a bit facing each other. The level is solved when the lit set holds every tile. We use the same walk to record each tile's distance from the core, which the renderer uses to make light spread outward along the pipes after a move.

You can play it in the browser at webgame.fun/game/lantern-links, with no sign-up. The whole game is one MIT-licensed file on GitHub, and it is also on itch.io. Open index.html and search for generate to find the code above. If you spot a bug in the generator, or a better way to pick the shape, an issue is welcome.

We are the WebGame.fun team, a small group making free browser games: webgame.fun.

Anthropic’s Backers Have Another Big Bet: Americans Staying Renters

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic’s stock market debut on Thursday is expected to value the artificial intelligence company at $1.5 trillion, putting some of Wall Street’s biggest investment firms in line to cash in from its rapid rise.

In its pitch to investors, the maker of Claude offers a vision far grander than the 2,800 millionaires the IPO is expected to mint. AI, it says, could “significantly accelerate global growth and dramatically improve quality of life for people around the world.”

But some of its backers are making another bet on a future where renting dominates the housing market.

Among them are Blackstone and Brookfield, two investment giants whose portfolios extend from emerging technologies to the nation's housing stock.

“Amidst continued macroeconomic uncertainty, we believe rental housing is the number one thing you want to own,” reads a 2025 shareholder update from Blackstone Real Estate Income Trust, known as BREIT.

Under a heading touting Blackstone Real Estate’s “Best Ideas,” the fund pointed to record homeownership costs, sluggish construction, and a national shortage of 4 million to 5 million homes. It called those conditions “powerful tailwinds” for its rental housing investments.

To housing advocates, though, those tailwinds are precisely the headwinds they’re working to reverse, says Jordan Ash, housing director at the nonprofit watchdog Private Equity Stakeholder Project.

The investment thesis anticipates a future in which “there are many more people who are just going to be renters for life,” Ash tells Realtor.com®.

“The renter is not their customer,” he says of large private equity landlords. “They're accountable to their investors.”

And now, the rental market is showing signs of turning in those investors’ favor.

Median asking rents rose 0.1% in October—the first annual increase since June 2023—across the 50 largest metros, according to Realtor.com research. The increase was small, but it came as the apartment construction boom that helped hold rents down began to fade.

Meanwhile, households that might otherwise leave the rental market face a steep price for becoming homeowners.

Mortgage rates averaged 7.33% in October, roughly double their 3.69% level in October 2019, notes Realtor.com senior economist Jiayi Xu.

“Elevated mortgage rates are one of the key barriers to homeownership, because they significantly raise the monthly cost of buying,” she explains. Higher ownership expenses have added to the burden.

That financial hurdle is apparent in Dallas–Fort Worth, TX, where Blackstone owns rental homes through Tricon, the operator it acquired in 2024.

Across the metro, the median monthly cost of buying a starter home was $2,741 in October, compared with $1,471 to rent, according to data from Realtor.com. That means making the leap to homeownership would involve finding an additional $1,270 in the monthly budget—after also coming up with a down payment.

It's easy to understand the bet then—with a gap that wide, who would ever be able to afford the jump?

Washington has tried to make that leap easier by limiting buyers’ competition from Wall Street. Following President Donald Trump’s January executive order, Congress enacted restrictions on additional single-family home purchases by institutional investors controlling at least 350 such homes. The ban takes effect on Jan. 7, 2027, with exceptions that include qualifying build-to-rent projects.

But limiting competition from investors does not, by itself, close the monthly gap between renting and owning. And while that gap gives households a reason to keep renting, BREIT expects declining construction to give landlords more room to raise rents.

“Ultimately, lower supply should support rent growth and real estate values over time,” the fund wrote in its Nov. 4 shareholder update, which did not address the institutional investor ban.

Of course, that outcome isn’t assured. Although the multifamily construction pipeline has shrunk from its peak, it remains large by pre-pandemic standards, Xu says. Projects already underway could continue giving tenants more options.

Even so, “If new multifamily deliveries cannot keep pace with rental demand, we expect rents to rise,” she says.

But even buying a home doesn't always end the relationship with a landlord.

Ash points to Brookfield’s acquisition of YES Communities, an operator of manufactured-home communities where residents can own their houses while paying rent for the land beneath them.

Residents’ complaints about rising costs and neglected maintenance predated Brookfield’s purchase. And as the deal approached, some appealed directly to pension funds invested with the firm, seeking assurances that a new owner would improve their circumstances.

“The rent just keeps going up, but no improvements have been made,” Michigan resident Abbie Pedrotte told University of California regents, according to Ash’s reporting.

By October, those concerns had reached Congress.

In an Oct. 13 letter, Rep. Rashida Tlaib (D-MI) and two colleagues pressed Brookfield about its plans. They asked whether it intended to raise home and lot rents by more than 3% annually and whether it would cooperate with residents seeking to purchase their parks. Written answers were requested by Nov. 20.

For its part, Brookfield’s published strategy calls for increasing resident homeownership and expanding tenant programs to “boost operating income and retention.” Owning the house can give a resident a stake in the community while preserving the landlord’s income from the land.

“We own the appreciating part of real estate. It’s fantastic,” Lowell Baron, CEO of Brookfield’s real estate business, told investors in September.

Some of the firms betting on continued demand for rentals are also financing a construction boom of their own.

BREIT had deployed more than $9 billion into data center developments in 2026 by its November shareholder update, helping expand the infrastructure supporting AI and cloud computing.

Blackstone has already identified a potential cost for other real estate—including homebuilding.

On its October earnings call, President Jon Gray said rising construction costs and limited new supply were being intensified by higher interest rates and “crowding out from the AI infrastructure build.”

A construction industry survey offers evidence of that competition. Among firms reporting on data center projects’ workforce effects, 58% cited increased competition for skilled workers, while 49% reported greater wage pressure, according to the 2026 AGC/NCCER Workforce Survey.

Xu cautions against treating those findings as a measure of the impact on homebuilding.

“The survey points to labor pressure where data centers are concentrated, but it does not isolate AI investment from factors like immigration enforcement and tariffs, nor does it directly measure residential construction costs,” she explains.

And to their credit, the private equity firms are also moving to help finance additional housing. Blackstone launched a homebuilder lending platform in May that it expects to support more than 50,000 new for-sale homes annually.

Buying existing homes changes ownership without adding to the housing stock, Xu explains.

“Financing new construction, by contrast, increases the stock, which is the key to expanding supply and improving affordability for both groups,” she says.

Anthropic’s backers have capital to help build the future they describe. Households waiting for a more affordable home need some of that future to include them.

Allaire Conte is a senior advice writer covering real estate and personal finance trends. She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

2 Monster Stocks to Hold for the Next 10 Years

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Both of these stocks have turned $10,000 into roughly $70,000 or more over the past decade, and their businesses are still built to keep compounding.

By John Ballard – Nov 19, 2026 at 4:45AM EST

The best stocks don't just beat the market for a year or two. They compound wealth for a decade or more, and that kind of return almost always traces back to a business that keeps growing its customer base, revenue, and profits.

MercadoLibre (MELI -0.95%) and Amazon (AMZN +3.82%) have both delivered monster returns, and their businesses are still growing, making them quality stocks to hold for 10 years.

A $10,000 investment in MercadoLibre 10 years ago would be worth about $94,000 today, a roughly 25% annualized return. However, the stock trades more than 40% below its June 2025 high because profits have stalled.

That is a deliberate choice, as management is prioritizing investments to strengthen the business for long-term growth. Management has lowered Brazil's free-shipping threshold several times since 2017, while ramping up credit card issuance and expanding fulfillment space nearly 20% last quarter.

It's short-term pain for long-term gain. Operating margin fell to 6.3% from 9.8% a year ago, even as revenue climbed 45% year over year. On the Nov. 4, 2026, earnings call, CEO Ariel Szarfsztejn said the company has "accepted a lower margin" to deepen customer relationships.

Those relationships are showing up in other metrics. Gross merchandise volume (GMV) rose 38% to $23 billion, unique active buyers grew 22% to 93.9 million, and purchase frequency hit a record.

The risk is that competition from Shopee and others keeps profits down longer than investors expect. Still, the economics are improving. Brazil's cost per shipment is down 14% since Q1 2025, and high-margin advertising grew 58%.

Analysts expect earnings per share (EPS) to jump 45% to $52.62 in 2027, according to Yahoo! Finance. At $1,491 as of Nov. 13, 2026, the stock trades at 28 times that estimate. That's a fair valuation for a quality e-commerce stock with excellent long-term growth prospects.

A $10,000 investment in Amazon 10 years ago would be worth about $70,000 today, a roughly 22% annualized return. The engine behind those gains is growth in operating income, which reached $104 billion over the past year, up 37% year over year, excluding foreign currency effects.

Amazon Web Services (AWS) is a fast-growing, high-margin business, with revenue up 39% to $46 billion last quarter, and a backlog of $578 billion. CEO Andy Jassy expects AWS to grow more than 40% in both 2027 and 2028. Amazon also plans to sell its Trainium AI chips outside AWS starting late 2027.

Amazon's core retail business is still growing, too. North America and international margins have each expanded nearly 10 percentage points over the past few years, and Amazon is still finding ways to lower costs that could make its online store even more profitable.

The risk is the spending required to support growth, particularly the addition of more data center capacity for AWS. Capital expenditures will reach about $220 billion in 2026 and rise "meaningfully" in 2027. But trailing-12-month operating cash flow still grew 29% to $168 billion, and Jassy says Amazon has a "clear line of sight to attractive financial returns."

Analysts expect EPS to increase from $7.17 in 2025 to $10.69 in 2027. At $266.66 as of Nov. 13, 2026, Amazon trades at about 25 times 2027 estimates. The stock should continue to compound in value as the business grows over the next 10 years.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 794%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 203% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

CoinShares unloads $19.3M in ETH and SOL, yet keeps crypto risk

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CoinShares sold $19.3 million in Ethereum and Solana to fund potential buybacks as crypto prices continue to shape its earnings.

CoinShares Chief Executive Jean-Marie Mognetti said on Nov. 18 that October’s sale of the firm’s entire corporate treasury Ethereum position and half its treasury Solana freed up $19.3 million for potential share repurchases.

The company’s third-quarter earnings show how its shareholders remain exposed to crypto prices through its remaining holdings and the fees it charges on investment products.

CoinShares raised $16.6 million from the Ethereum sale and $2.7 million from Solana. Together, they amounted to about 48% of the board’s $40 million repurchase authorization.

The board approved the program on Oct. 22, and CoinShares announced it on Oct. 27, with a run time until the next annual meeting, expected in the second quarter of 2027. Purchases depend on market conditions, blackout periods, and trading-volume limits.

Before the sale, CoinShares held $43.2 million in treasury digital assets, including 235 Bitcoin worth $19.6 million, as of Sept. 30, according to its interim financial statements.

The treasury also held $17.6 million in Ethereum and $5.9 million in Solana at that date. The October disclosure identified sales of Ethereum and Solana, with half the Solana position retained.

The filing separately lists $4.1 billion of digital assets held for operations as of Sept. 30, including $880.5 million of Ethereum and $407.8 million of Solana. Some of those assets back exchange-traded products or hedge its obligations to product holders.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

CoinShares announced the October sale before releasing its Q3 accounts on Nov. 18. The two disclosures show where shareholders still carry crypto risk.

The treasury sale reduced direct token holdings. Crypto prices still affect fees, and lending exposes the business to borrower defaults.

The loan balance predates the sale. The reviewed disclosures give no precise post-sale treasury value or count of shares repurchased.

The filled bar is $19.3 million of reported October proceeds divided by the $40 million board ceiling, or 48.25%, rounded to about 48% in the label. Product fees are for Q3 2026. Digital assets on loan are net of the allowance for expected credit losses at Sept. 30, before the October treasury sale.

The loan balance predates the sale. The reviewed disclosures give no precise post-sale treasury value or count of shares repurchased.

Assets under management climbed from $5.5 billion at the June close to $7.4 billion at the September close.

The $125 million in quarterly net inflows accounted for about 7% of the increase, with price appreciation adding roughly $1.75 billion, based on CoinShares’ first-half and nine-month figures. At its CoinShares Physical platform alone, prices added $938.4 million and net inflows added $77.8 million.

CoinShares reported that management fees held at $19.1 million in both quarters. The fees accrue on daily assets, and the late-quarter rally lifted the September closing balance more than the average fee base.

The total also includes seed assets that pay no fees, with CoinShares reporting $6.4 billion in fee-paying assets at quarter-end.

The asset manager had $358.1 million of digital assets on loan as of Sept. 30, after accounting for expected credit losses, up from $69.8 million at the end of 2025. Some loans are unsecured, so the firm can lose money if a borrower defaults.

Management said it shifted Ethereum from staking into lending during the third quarter, and the loan book earned $4.3 million in interest that quarter, alongside $2.8 million in staking revenue.

Stronger lending and trading lifted capital markets segment revenue and gains to $17.8 million from $4.9 million in the second quarter.

The third-quarter segment figure comprised $8.6 million in revenue and $9.2 million in operating gains. CoinShares reported $27.7 million in consolidated revenue, including asset management fees.

An unrealized $9.6 million gain came from the gap between the value of cash-redeemable XBT certificates and the assets CoinShares holds to hedge them. The gain was reflected in the company’s $28.4 million of operating income and excluded from the $17.8 million segment figure.

A $14.4 million treasury revaluation gain was recorded below operating income and helped lift net income to $39.6 million.

CoinShares said its expanded monthly tracker adds lending, staking and treasury figures to the existing product assets and flows data.

That gives investors a way to track what the company holds and what it puts to work, while measuring buyback execution separately by the shares the company reports purchasing.

Arsenal have five first-team central midfielders - but want to sign a sixth

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Arsenal have entered the race for Alex Scott, with Bournemouth softening their stance in selling their prize asset.

The Guernsey-born schemer emerged as one of the Cherries' key players last season under Andoni Iraola, starting the season impressively and earning his first England cap earlier this season.

With a plethora of big clubs interested in his signature, however, his future may well lie away from the south coast.

Arsenal have no pressing concerns to strengthen in midfield, having already picked up Bruno Guimaraes over the summer to compete with Martin Zubimendi alongside Declan Rice.

Manager Mikel Arteta also has Myles Lewis-Skelly and Mikel Merino as options in the midfield, too, meaning that any incoming signing would be competing with four other players for a spot alongside Rice and Martin Odegaard.

Sky Sports have reported, however, that this hasn't stopped the champions from joining the race for the Bournemouth star, alongside Chelsea and Liverpool.

With Scott out of contract in 2028, the Cherries are under pressure to either extend his deal or sell him while they can still fetch a big fee – but would rather not sell midseason, as they had to when Manchester City triggered Antoine Semenyo's release clause in January.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Liverpool are believed to be leading the race, given the link with now-manager Andoni Iraola, while Chelsea are also retain interest from the summer, having failed to land Lamine Camara towards the end of the summer window.

Arsenal will naturally have to sell one of their midfield options to land Scott, with both Merino and Zubimendi struggling for game time this term, with the former speaking out recently about both his and his Basque team-mate's lack of minutes this season.

“We know what our roles are, and we're both calm and eager to keep training hard to show that we can continue to be there,” Merino said, adding, “Not being on the pitch most of the time isn't ideal for a player. Arsenal is currently the best team in the world, and it's very difficult to get minutes.”

Lewis-Skelly could well be an option to sell, however, with the Hale End star representing a pure profit sale, should he depart.

The same Sky Sports report adds that Arsenal are also looking to the likes of RB Leipzig's Antonio Nusa and Brentford's Kevin Schade to strengthen the attack.

Scott is worth €70 million, according to Transfermarkt. Arsenal travel to Newcastle United when Premier League action resumes this weekend.

Kiribati, il Paese che sfida il cambiamento climatico per un sogno chiamato calcio

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Un arcipelago di 34 tra atolli e isole coralline affiora a malapena dall’acqua, superando di rado i due metri sul livello del mare: questo è Kiribati, uno degli Stati più remoti, bassi e vulnerabili del pianeta. Con una popolazione di circa 135 mila abitanti distribuiti su tre gruppi di isole (le Gilbert, dove si concentra il 90% della popolazione e la capitale Tarawa Sud, le Isole della Fenice e le Sporadi Equatoriali), Kiribati è una nazione di unicità straordinarie ma anche di sfide quotidiane ed essenziali per la propria sopravvivenza. E’ il primo Paese al mondo a inaugurare il nuovo anno, grazie a un'estensione geografica che attraversa tre fusi orari e lambisce tutti e quattro gli emisferi (boreale, australe, orientale e occidentale). La sua bandiera racconta l'anima di questo popolo: il sole che sorge dall'oceano con 17 raggi (rappresentanti le 16 isole Gilbert e l'isola Banaba) e la fregata minore (‘te eitei’ in lingua locale), simbolo di libertà e orgoglio. Dietro la bellezza di questi angoli turchesi si cela però un'urgenza, quella del cambiamento climatico, e una passione irrefrenabile per il calcio.

Parlare di Kiribati oggi significa fare i conti con una delle trincee in cui si combattono gli effetti del cambiamento climatico. L'intero Paese rischia infatti di scomparire a causa dell'innalzamento del livello dei mari e del riscaldamento globale. Gli atolli dipendono interamente dalle falde sotterranee di acqua dolce e l'acqua salata sta progressivamente inquinando queste riserve, rendendo sempre più difficile l'approvvigionamento idrico. Il fenomeno non è teorico, anzi: già nel 1999 due isolotti disabitati, Tebua Tarawa e Abanuea, sono stati interamente sommersi. Secondo il “Rapporto speciale sull’oceano e la criosfera nell’ambito del cambiamento climatico” il livello medio globale del mare è destinato a crescere ulteriormente nei prossimi decenni: per una nazione così pianeggiante e la cui superficie terrestre totale è di appena 811 chilometri quadrati ciò si traduce in inondazioni, erosione costiera e una progressiva riduzione dello spazio vitale per una popolazione che deve già fare i conti con il sovraffollamento della capitale Tarawa Sud. In questo scenario di estrema vulnerabilità ambientale e sociale - segnato da opportunità limitate per i giovani in termini di svago, istruzione e sviluppo - emerge con forza il ruolo della Kiribati Islands Football Federation (KIFF) guidata dal Presidente Eriati Reebo. “Per i giovani di Kiribati il calcio non è mero svago, ma uno strumento di riscatto e coesione sociale”, ha raccontato a Tuttomercatoweb. “La KIFF utilizza il calcio per insegnare disciplina, lavoro di squadra, rispetto e leadership, offrendo un'alternativa concreta ai ragazzi disimpegnati e promuovendo attivamente l'inclusione delle donne, delle ragazze e delle persone con disabilità. È un collante che unisce le famiglie, le imprese locali e le isole più remote, creando un forte senso di identità nazionale”.

Il cambiamento climatico sta già adesso condizionando i programmi calcistici del Paese. “E’ una delle sfide più grandi che Kiribati deve affrontare - prosegue Eriati Reebo - e il calcio non è immune ai suoi impatti. Siamo un Paese particolarmente vulnerabile e tali emergenze stanno influenzando la nostra capacità di organizzare e sostenere le attività calcistiche: le forti piogge possono lasciare i campi da calcio allagati e inadatti alle partite o agli allenamenti. Il caldo estremo pone anche seri rischi per la salute e la sicurezza dei giocatori, in particolare dei bambini. Per Kiribati gli ostacoli sono ancora maggiori a causa delle nostre limitate infrastrutture sportive, per questo abbiamo urgente bisogno di un sostegno internazionale per sviluppare strutture calcistiche sicure, resilienti e sostenibili. Per questo motivo, la KIFF ritiene che la resilienza climatica debba diventare parte della nostra strategia di sviluppo calcistico a lungo termine”.

Nonostante le difficoltà geografiche, climatiche ed economiche, la Federazione sta lavorando alacremente per ottenere il riconoscimento internazionale. Attualmente, la KIFF è membro associato della Confederazione Calcistica dell'Oceania (OFC), ma non può partecipare alle qualificazioni per la Coppa del Mondo FIFA, limitando le proprie apparizioni ufficiali a tornei regionali. La Nazionale fino al 2012 aveva giocato solo 11 partite: la prima, nel 1979, contro le Figi, si concluse con una sconfitta per 24-0 in una partita dei Giochi del Pacifico meridionale. Dopo alcuni anni di inattività nel 2016 la KIFF si è unita alla Conifa. Poi, nello scorso ottobre, a 47 anni dal proprio debutto, Kiribati ha vinto la sua prima partita in assoluto, superando per 6-0 contro il Nadroga FC, un club della Fiji Premier League: la Nazionale guidata da Pine George Holomoana Iosefa si è laureata pochi giorni dopo campione della Premier Division nell'86° FMF Inter-District Championship, battendo il Seaqaqa FC 6-1 in finale all'HFC Bank Stadium di Suva. Nel 2027 è in programma la Four Nations Cup contro Tuvalu, Tonga e Isole Cook e tramite il sito ufficiale della Federazione (kiri.football) vi è la possibilità di aiutare la KIFF a raccogliere fondi in vista degli imminenti impegni anche tramite l’acquisto delle divise ufficiali della Nazionale. Recentemente il presidente della Federcalcio locale ha incontrato il presidente della FIFA Gianni Infantino: “E’ stata un’importante opportunità per condividere le nostre aspirazioni e spiegare le sfide e il potenziale dello sviluppo calcistico a Kiribati. La nostra ambizione è ottenere la piena adesione alla OFC e alla FIFA. Comprendiamo che l'adesione non è automatica e che ci sono requisiti, procedure e standard di governance che la KIFF deve soddisfare, ma la popolazione di Kiribati merita le stesse opportunità delle altre nazioni calcistiche. Saremo anche un piccolo Paese insulare, ma la nostra passione per il calcio e le nostre ambizioni sono grandi quanto quelle di qualsiasi altra nazione”. Poi ci sono le urgenze relative ai club. “Una delle sfide che dobbiamo affrontare – prosegue - è la necessità di rafforzare e standardizzare la nostra competizione nazionale, migliorando l'amministrazione dei club, gli allenamenti e la formazione degli arbitri. L'obiettivo della KIFF è sviluppare un sistema calcistico nazionale più organizzato e sostenibile che offra competizioni regolari per uomini, donne e giovani”. La KIFF Pro League è il livello più alto del calcio maschile per club a Kiribati: lanciata nel 2026, comprende 8 club in League 1 e 6 in League 2.

La visione della KIFF per i prossimi anni poggia su cinque pilastri: la piena adesione a OFC e FIFA, il rafforzamento del calcio giovanile e femminile, la costruzione di infrastrutture resilienti al clima (incluso un vero e proprio stadio nazionale), lo sviluppo tecnico e l'inclusione sociale totale. Come ripete spesso la federazione attraverso il suo mantra — "Think Positive" ("Pensa in positivo") —, il calcio a Kiribati può “fungere da potente piattaforma per aumentare la consapevolezza internazionale sulle realtà del cambiamento climatico nelle piccole nazioni insulari”, spiega il numero uno della Federcalcio locale. In un Paese che rischia di sparire dalle cartine geografiche, il calcio è a tutti gli effetti una prova di (r)esistenza.

Bitcoin Bounces From $62K: Is Gold and Silver’s 14% Crash Sending Money Into Crypto?

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

BTC bounced from $62K as silver fell 14% since Nov 9. What does fund trading reveal, and is there really a shift from metals into Bitcoin?

In today’s Bitcoin news, on November 19, silver and gold are extending their sell-off, but Bitcoin is holding its ground near $63,800. BTC has bounced off the $62,000 level for the second time in four days, raising the question of whether crypto’s sell-off is finally losing steam and investors are starting to move money back into digital assets.

Silver futures closed Wednesday at $45 an ounce, down 4.8% on the day and 14% since November 9, while gold fell another 3.2%.

JUST IN: $1,400,000,000,000 wiped out from Gold and Silver in the past 24 hours. pic.twitter.com/NwLlK9b1iv

— Whale Insider (@WhaleInsider) November 19, 2026

For traders following Bitcoin news, the obvious explanation is that money is moving out of metals and into crypto. We tested that by comparing how much money changes hands each day in the three largest US funds for silver, gold, and Bitcoin. If investors were switching, Bitcoin’s slice of that trading would grow.

Well, for now it hasn’t: Bitcoin took 30% of the money traded over the past five trading days, about the same as the 31% it took over the 20 days before.

Bitcoin’s bounce also came with less trading than the sell-off before it, a sign that buyers are still cautious.

So is this the start of a shift from metals to crypto, or just a pause in the selling?

Exchange-traded funds (ETFs) give a clean view of where US money goes, because they trade side by side on the same exchanges. We took the three biggest: the iShares Silver Trust (SLV), SPDR Gold Shares (GLD), and BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT). For each, we multiplied the closing price by the shares traded to get the dollars that changed hands each day.

So the money leaving silver and gold isn’t showing up in Bitcoin funds, at least not yet. It looks more like a broad sell-off in both, with metals now taking the heavier hit.

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Best Low-Cap Crypto

Binance’s BTCUSDT pair tells the same Bitcoin news story from the crypto side. During the drop from November 13 to 15, it traded an average of $2.0 billion a day. In the three days since, as Bitcoin held above $62,000, the average fell to $1.5 billion.

Lighter trading on a bounce means fewer buyers are stepping in. Sellers have slowed down, but buyers haven’t rushed back.

Bitcoin’s spot ETFs, which hold the coin itself, show a similar picture. IBIT took in $76.5 million on November 16, its first inflow after 11 days of outflows. That’s a start, but one day of buying after two weeks of selling.

What drives both markets is the same: the US Federal Reserve. Traders expect another rate hike in December, and higher rates hurt assets that pay no interest, from silver to Bitcoin. Until that changes, a rotation from one to the other has little to feed on.

EXPLORE: Bitcoin ETF Inflows Return After Seven-Day Losing Streak as IBIT Adds $76.5M: Is the Sell-Off Finally Over?

On the daily chart, Bitcoin trades at $63,713. That is below its 50-day exponential moving average (EMA), an average that gives recent prices more weight, at $75,245, and its 200-day EMA at $75,624.

The Bitcoin news to watch is volume. A move above $65,500 with trading back near $2 billion a day would be a start. If IBIT’s share of fund trading also climbs above a third, money is actually moving from metals to crypto.

Stock Market Sell-Off: 3 Investing Steps I'm Taking Right Now, and 1 Costly Mistake I'm Avoiding

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It’s getting difficult to ignore the market’s warnings. Just don’t fall into the trap of overreacting.

By James Brumley – Nov 19, 2026 at 4:30AM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

For a short while following the market's steep sell-off on Nov. 5, it looked like stocks might be able to shrug it off and rekindle the advance underway since late 2022. Now, not so much. The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.23%) just fell under that terrible day's low, hitting its lowest level since mid-June. Rattled by the worry that it could happen, investors may end up creating the very correction they fear by dumping the stocks they're not truly committed to for the long haul.

I get it, by the way. And, though I don't believe we're on the cusp of a new bear market, I do believe any correction right now will also mark a change in this bull market's tone and timbre. What worked well in the recent past may not work as well in the foreseeable future, and vice versa.

And yes, although it was bound to happen sooner or later for one reason or another, I believe a sweeping reset of artificial intelligence stocks' valuations is driving much of this change. In that light, the cooldown of AI mania may actually be of some long-term benefit.

Whatever the case, here's a rundown of three steps I'm taking right now to protect my portfolio, and a closer look at the one thing I'm absolutely not doing.

Most of the time, investors do a pretty good job of keeping their goals and risk tolerances in mind when deciding which stocks to own and which ones to avoid. Even the best of us, however, occasionally let hope or a compelling story lure us into a trade we normally wouldn't make. I'm scouring my portfolio for names like this, and have found a couple of tickers I don't think will bounce back all that well even when the broad market does. You might want to do the same.

Here's the litmus test for every stock you own: Is this a name you actually want to hold on to through the next bear market so you own it at the beginning of the next bull market (whenever those might be)? If not, go ahead and shed it now even if you're doing so at a loss. That's tough to do, but the worse things get from here, the tougher any bounce-back becomes for these questionable names.

This won't prevent the rest of my portfolio from being dragged lower with the rest of the market, to be clear, if that's what's in the cards. I just won't care, knowing that any such setback is merely temporary.

I'm eyeing any further market weakness as a buying opportunity, in fact. The thing is, you must be prepared to act well ahead of time, in a couple of ways.

One of these ways is simply to determine which companies you'd like to own at the beginning of the next recovery, with plans to hold on to those names indefinitely once you're in. The challenge here is that very few stocks look all that compelling while they're sinking. This is why you'll want to build a watch list of publicly traded companies you'd like to own without any concern about their stocks' recent performance. With that mindset, it's much easier to jump in when it feels like everybody else is getting out.

Another related way to ensure you're prepared to pounce on opportunities when they appear is to make sure you've got enough cash available when that window opens. You don't want to make decisions about selling a holding to free up cash under duress, simply because we don't always make smart decisions when stressed.

You'll probably want to have more ideas on your watch list than you can realistically step into, by the way, since some of them may never sink to a price that's too good to pass up.

Perhaps one of the biggest mistakes many investors make is failing to consider which sectors and industries are well-positioned to thrive at any given time.

Don't misunderstand; you always want to maintain at least a little exposure to most of the market's 11 major sectors. But there are times when you'll want to make a point of owning a piece of a certain sliver of stocks, or avoiding a particular group. For instance, AI stocks were must-haves between 2023 and now, but their recent weakness may indicate that AI mania's best days are behind it. At the other end of the spectrum, while consumer staples may have lagged from 2023 to 2025 simply because they weren't exciting enough to keep investors interested, these names have been attracting attention in 2026 due to investors' perceived need for safety.

I'm thinking about this now because -- as was noted -- I think the bull market from here is going to look and feel very different than the bull market up until now.

Finally, the one thing I'm absolutely not doing here is panicking to the point of dumping everything (or even just too much) in fear of what might happen. My fear isn't actually suffering losses, however. I've done that before, and survived. My fear is not being fully invested when I know I want to be. That's at the very beginning of a new bull market, or even just the very beginning of a bounce-back from a correction.

Numbers from mutual fund company Hartford tell the tale. The investment manager says the S&P 500 averages a 13.6% gain in just the first month of a new bull market, and 25.3% in its first three months. Compared to the average gain of 114% for the entirety of the average bull market, you can't really afford to be on the sidelines for any of it, even if it means being in during the bear market before it.

Again, I'm not saying we're entering a bear market. We could be, but I doubt it. If we were going to, we should have done so by now. I've got a feeling the domestic and global economy are going to find a way to wiggle out of this weird economic lull without going through a recession and subsequent market weakness. My purpose in pointing out Hartford's data is simply to illustrate that the market can and does move quickly in unexpected ways at unexpected times. We have to remain prepared for all possibilities, which means staying in the market even when doing so feels uncomfortable.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 794%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 203% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

Most people skip this exercise, but it's essential for strong abs and hips over 60 — and it's not crunches, sit-ups, or planks

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As you get older, a strong core becomes even more important to help you maintain balance and stability and avoid injury. But the types of core exercises you enjoyed at 20 might not be the types you enjoy today, and for me personally, at 35, I now prefer low-impact, deep core exercises over typical moves like sit-ups, crunches, or planks.

For that reason, I wanted to share one exercise I love for strengthening your core and hips while remaining low-impact for your spine.

One of the best core exercises I use with my dad, who is nearing 73, looks simple but really blasts your core and deep stabilizer muscles. It also strengthens your hip flexors, which are important muscles for walking and running. Read on to learn more.

As a reminder, this exercise is beginner-friendly. Still, if you're pregnant, postpartum, or dealing with an injury, it’s always best to seek personalized advice from an expert who knows you and your body.

I like exercises that keep your spine straight and stable while teaching the various muscles that form your core to maintain and control tension. So, rather than peddling out endless sit-ups, which flex your spine over and over, I recommend trying leg openers.

If you're unsure what "bracing" your core means, I teach you how with a 5-minute bracing routine.

Here’s how to perform leg openers with proper form:

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

A post shared by Tom's Guide Fitness | Workout Tips & Gear (@tomsguidefitness)

Avoid hunching or dumping into your torso. Imagine a piece of string running along your spine and coming out of the crown of your head. Now imagine someone pulling that string to lift you.

Move slowly and with control, using your braced stomach to maintain your upright position. You can slightly lean back, but try not to do so excessively.

Yes, which is why I use it with older clients regularly. The move teaches proper posture and spinal alignment as you keep your shoulders stacked over your hips and avoid hunching or rounding. This also requires your core muscles to activate to hold you upright and keep you stable; a strong, active core protects your lower back.

Leg openers also work your hip flexors, which are important muscles for lifting your knees, allowing you to walk and run. As you abduct the hip, your outer glutes and adductors also switch on; your glutes help stabilize the pelvis, and the adductors run along your inner thighs, sometimes referred to as the groin.

This exercise is much friendlier for your lower back than moves like sit-ups, as you can support your lower back if needed, and you don't need to use any extreme range of motion, like hyperextension or flexion, or spinal compression. This move also teaches you to control the movement you generate, increasing time under tension, or how long your muscles work, to build endurance.

I like to count four seconds out with a one-second pause and a four-second count back in. If you notice you're leaning back too much, position yourself against a wall to prevent this, as you want to maintain a strong L-shaped position with your body. A soft bend in the working leg is fine if you have tight hamstrings.
